• vv... iaia,..fieeaen no great searching—a glance 'showed him there was no one excepting himself there; but the open fanlight—had anyone else been there, or had the wind of the night opened it? He switched off the light, mounted a chair, closed and bolted it, then once more he turned on the electric light. It would be better not to mention his half-suspicion that someone had entered— suspicion which would be almost ridiculous. Who but a harlequin could enter by a fanlight? He thought of what the man, Clark, had said of the escaped prisoner; he had left the prison , without leaving any trace of his escape behind. , Somehow or other, he had hung on to a perpen- dicular wall while he had replaced bars and , °ricks. Was it possible he had enterea tne house through the fanlight? , "Anyhow, he is not here nwv," John said to himself. "If he has been inlhis room he has Left it, which is really all that can concern me." He shut the door, then he looked about him. ' The first thing almost upon which his eyes fell ' was a walking-stick; a stout, cumbersome stick of ebony, the handle formed from a hippopotamus tusk. It was not his; it must have belonged to his brother. / I He took it up and examined it. There was no name upon it, yet anyone looking at it would have said at once it belonged to someone who had travelled the world over, not to a stay-at- home like himself. He looked round, wondering where he could put it. Some swords—swords that had been used by dead and gone ancestors —were placed in a horizontal position on the wall, supported by brass brackets. John took up the walking-stick and balanced it above them; it did not look any way out of place—it might have been there for years. There was nothing else about the room that might have told of his brother's presence. He sat down and waited. It was very curious, hat he felt no remorse or , even horror of what he had done. He liardly seemed to realise anything excepting that Diek , was out of the way—not alive to interfere with him in any way !or the future. The horror that ,as a rule overwhelms an educated person who has wilfully and maliciously taken a life, did not' touch him. His passion for Celia Verde; as it were, swamped and obliterated everything else, every other consideration. If on thgleve of his marriage half a dozen had stood betvF6en him and his bride, he would have killed them if possible. It is an absolute fact that nothing makes a man more fierce, more cruel, than anything that comes between him and his most tender passion. John Brandon sat down and listened for the re-coming of the prison warders. They had reached, no doubt, the top of the house now, ' for he could hear nothing of them; but by-and- bye there was a thumping on the staircase. John wished the clever prisoner had chosen any other night to escape; it seemed to him he should never get to bed. It rejoiced him that the morn- ing was growing nearer and nearer, but it would have come so much more quickly had he been asleep. He heard footsteps at last; the search-party going down to the basement, so they had drawn blank so far. Ho would have to wait another half-hour, perhaps, before they came to him. He looked at the time, it was almost two o'clock. He had meant to be up with the lark. He touched the bell. His servant answered it. "Have they found anyone?" he asked. In deference to his master, the man suppressed a grin. "Nothing, sir," he answered. "They're behaving in my opinion—begging your pardon— most ridiculous, looking, one might almost say, in every pill-box." John Brandon had smiled slightly. Truth to tell, he was too tired to feel very much amused. "They won't find anyone here," he said. "Is it likely? The man, even if he came this way, would have seen the family was awake, still about He may be in the outhouses; he most certainly isn't in the house." Robert nodded. "That's what I tell them, sir," I he answered, "but they've got their own theory. I The man is desperately clever, daring, so they I say; there is nothing in the world they don't think he's capable of. He's nearly escaped, I it seems, twice before. Ah l they are on this landing now " John Brandon leant forward in his chair, listen- ing. He could hear the men talking, the sound of their footsteps. He thought they seemed dis- appointed, but that might only have been his fancy. Anyway, he was glad they had not found the man on his premises. He didn't want to be I dragged into any prison affair. He sat listening. He heard the search-party enter tho drawing-room, the dining-room, the morning, billiard, and smoking-rooms, then there was a knock at the door of the room in which he sat. A little fear came back to him though he told ; himself he was foolish to be afraid. The safe was locked. Nothing—nothing under Heaven would induce him to unlock it, even should he . be askeil to, which was not likely. He called out "Come in," and the men entered. "Well ? " John said, 'the one word a question. "Nothing so far," Warder Clark answered. "But I don't despair. I feel sure, absolutely sure, he's in this house. We traced, almost saw him within twenty yards of it. It won't disturb you if I have a look round here, Mr. I3randon ? " John Brandon fought with a desire, a perfectly suicidal desire, to look at the safe, and conquered it. 4"Not in the least," he answered; "but since I've been here all the evening, and there is not a hiding-place in the room in which a mouse' could conceal itself, I don't think it is much use." Warder Clark winked slowly. "You've not been absolutely in the room all the evening," he said. "You were not in it, for instance, when we came here. A room that had just betn occupied is just the place in which a man would choose to hide himself. Still, as you say, it does not look very promising." He came a step or two further into the room and looked round. The whole place was absolutely open to inspection. There were two , tables in it, both innocent of cloths; there were I no cupboards, no recesses. There was absolutely no place in which, seemingly, a man could hide himself. Warder Clark went to the French window and tried it. "This has been shut all the even- ing?" he asked. "Yes," John answered. "And bolted? I ask because, of course, a French window is very much more accessible than any other if only latched." "And bolted," John returned, telling a story, he hardly knew why. Warder Clark glanced upward. "And the fanlight, is that made fast in any way?" John pointed to a bolt. "As you see," he answered.

(Th be continued.)

"That has been kept like that all the evening?" . John Brandon felt justified in telling another story. "Ever since tho first gun went." "Ali! so far as we know that may have been a I good hour after the man escaped. We had no rdes, anything was wrong until the W c h looked into his cell, thought there seemed/ something t strange about the figure in his bed, an absence of movement, and on investigating found how we had all been fooled. Still, there is not much , chance of cover here." He half-turned towards ' the door, then swung round again. "How about that 9" he said, and pointed to the safe. John Brandon's heart stood still. He knew he turned ashen white. A great fear overwhelmed him ; his very soul sickened with terror. If they should look there, if they should insist upon looking there, what then? But they should not; he had the key—they should not! He waited a moment, so that he could be sure of controlling his voice. "That safe?" ho said. "Oh, it is quite impossible anyone could be hidden there. I locked it myself. Besides, there is no handle inside, no way of shutting it. You may rest comfortable with regard to the safe." Warder Clark appeared not to notice the signs of fear on John Brandon's face. Ho laughed lightly. "Nothing is impossible to the' man we are after," he said. "Like love, Gentleman Jim will 'find a way. I think I must look in that safe. , It appears quite large enough for a man to hide In. A sort of paralysis fell upon John Brandon. His tongue clung to the roof of his mouth, a 'horrible cold sweat broke out upon him. He was afraid, horribly afraid, but he would not givo up the key, of course. "I'm afraid I cannot allow that," he said. , "It's quite impossible anyone can he hidden ' there, but there are private concerns of mine locked away in that safe. I cannot open it before anyone. If—if you care to go out of the room and leave it I will open it and investigate—though to do so is pure folly—but I won't allow anyone ek,

so may nave-men our quaririndden ' fliers; Seems to me, if it had been only a woman, he'd have tipped you the wink. I'm always a bit in. lined to be suanirinus. I know. but it looks queer to me. No. 27, if you remember, was a gentleman born and bred, though he was doing a ten years' stretch." . _

Chief Warder Green drew himself up. "He was a gentleman born and bred, as you say," he answered. "But he wasn't of these parts. We've got on a wrong scent, and may as well acknow- ledge it. As for the safe, I'm just as certain as though I had seen her with my two eyes, that a woman had bees hidden there. I don't wish to brag about it, but I've a sort of second 'sight about these things not given to most. My mother was seventh child of a seventh child, and I was her seventh son. That sets cow apart, so to speak, from other folk, and makes you see more."

"Pity it doesn't shew you where our prisoner is," someone said, in a slightly scoffing tone. "First sight is good enough for me, if second sight don't do more than prove to you that a safe is empty when you see it empty. There may be points about it, but I confess my vision isn't keen enough to see them; and not being an abnormal prodigy, I'll keep my own opinion." "You're welcome to it," Chief Warder Clark returned. "No one else wants to rob you of it, I'm sure. I—Hush ! What was that? I heard a sound, a rustle in the bushes over there. Our prisoner, or I'm a Dutchman! Now, quickly and carefully. He'll make for the cover yonder. You that can run swiftly, cut him off, if possible; if not, we must surround the place, and close in is the morning."

WRITTEN IN SCARLET.

• By ALICE MAUD MEADOWS, Author of "One Life Between," "A Million if Money," "The Extreme Penalty,' "For Lev* of a Lady," "I Charge Ton Both," 4to.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: It is the wedding eve of John Brandon, a well-to-do country gentleman, and Celia Verde, daughter of Lady Caroline Verde. Celia had been engaged to John's brother Dick, a wild and reckless young man, supposed to have died abroad. Celia admits that she loved her old sweetheart better than the new— in fact, that she does not love John at all. He, however, adoring her, promises himself that he will win her love after marriage. Then all is changed by the sudden reappearance of Dick, who meets his brother as he is going homewards. No other person has seen the wanderer except Lady Caroline's maid, who takes him for a ghost. John Brandon lets his brother in by the library window, unseen by his servants. Dick laughs at the idea of John marrying Celia, who, he is certain, still loves himself. His careless taunts excite John to fury, and he strikes his brother violently on the head with a ruler, intending only to disable him until after the wedding takes place. To his horror, however, the signs of , death are manifest. With quiet resolution he places the body In a large safe. At that moment the police arrive at the house in search of a convict who has just escaped from a neighbouring prison.

===== 0002601_19061201_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER V 1.3 EMPTY!

The request—almost demand—to search his house could not have come to John Brandon at a more inconvenient time. In the first place he was anxious to get to bed; in the second, a murdered man was locked in the great safe in his study. There was no fear, of course, that they would have the slightest suspicion of that; still, the situation was dangerous—to say the least of it.

He was perfectly conscious, too, that he felt nervous; probably, also, he looked nervous. He could not remember whether anything of Dick's had been left about the room; whether he had carried a stick. He could remember tossing his hat into the safe, but had there been anything else? Anything that might betray him? Later on, someone might say that they had see= him— the girl, for instance, whom Dick' had said had run screaming away. He might be missing, and any little clue connect his disappearance with him, John. _ _

Everyone had, of course, known that Celia Verde had once been engaged to his brother, that the love she had had for her first lover had been- far stronger, more passionate than her affection for him. Once the hue and cry got about that he had retprned, been seen, and then disappeared on the eve of John Brandon's wedding-day, suspicion would be awakened, and might—naturally would—grow doubly strong if anything of Dick's was found about. The voice of one of the prison warders—a man who seemed to speak with authority and had evidently been put above the others, or by length of service, or excellence of mind, occupied that position— interrupted Jon Brandon's thoughts. "We have placed a guard outside, sir," he said. "If he's in the house—and we think he is—we ,shall catch him; every door and window is watched. Have we your permission to go through the house?" .

John Brandon nodded. The menservants had risen and come downstairs; most of them looked sleepy and cross. Green, the butler, stepped forward. "I saw to all the bolts and bars myself before I went to bed," he said. "We heard the guns booming, and took precautions, so unless you find a door or window open, you may as well save yourselves the trouble of searching here." The chief warder laughed. "I don't know so much about that," he answered. "You don't know Gentleman Jim—that's what we couldn't help calling No. 27 he's done an amazing thing—simply amazing! It isn't a case of bolting while engaged on outside work when a sudden thick mist has fallen—that's how they generally get away—he's escaped from his cell, and on first inspection he's left no mode of egress behind him, nothing to betray his absence, and a dummy figure was made upon the bed. Some bars and bricks have been removed and replaced from the outside, where, at first sight, it seemed he had nothing to hang on to. A man like that would make his way into a house, and, leaving no trace of his ingress, lock it up after him. I tell you we must look into every hole and crarrny, every closed chest and cupboard. This man is flown, and we've got to catch him. I think we shall find our bird in this covey." John Brandon turned paler than ever; the words of the prison warder frightened him horribly. "We must search every hole and cranny, every locked chest and cupboard." Would they search the safe? If so, he might just as well go and blow out his brains; there would be no marriage for him to-morrow; the girl he loved would never, never be his wife. A halter, instead of the altar, would be his fate.

For all his fear, he schoOled his voice. He would, perhaps, be able to keep these men out of his study; he could very well, and quite truthfully, say he had been sitting there all the evening, that it was impossible anyone could have entered without his knowledge. "Where will you begin your search ? "ho asked. "I am practically sure the man is not here. It is hardly likely he would hide in a house— that would be too suicidal, too foolish; still, you are, of course, at liberty to search. Shall the servants show you to the basement ? " The menservants made a movement; the chief warder, whose name Was Clark, did not move. In answer to John Brandon's question, he shook his head.

"In searching a house," he said, "it is policy always to begin "'at the top—the roof, if there is any mode of egress to the roof—and beat down half -way ; it is also policy to send another beating party to the basement, not to beat upwards, but to remain there. Escape for anyone in hiding is then made almost impossible. We have your permission to go upstairs, Mr. Brandon ? "

John Brandon nodded. Ile did not feel it incumbent upon him to go with these men—they would do their searching ahme; besides, he must keep guard in the library, there must be no meddling there. "Go where you like, he answered. "I'm quite sure you are wasting time here; still, you think differently, and we all, I suppose, have a right to our opinions. If you want me, I shall be in my study. I have been there all the evening. Any of the servants will ihew you the way." He felt calmer for the moment; the search of the library, even if it came to that, was post- poned. When the warders found him seated there, in a room which was almost bare, without nook or cranny, or draperies, they would glance round and go away. Ile had no need to be alarmcd—none whatever. He saw the men disappear up the wide 8 tair- case, the servants with them; then he turned ;into the library, and switched on the light. .4-Directly he entered it, something strange, different, in the atmosphere struck him. When he had left it it had been hot and dry—so much so that for a minute hti had thought of opening the window; now it was cool and moist. The holland blinds edged with thick, handsome lace were dawn; he crossed to the window, and pulled the centre one up. The moon now shone vividly; the light in the garden was fairly bright—at all events, one could see perfectly. Half-way across the lawn John Brandon saw the figure of a man standing motionless watching the house, and a little further on another and another.

He tried the window, it was firmly shut though not bolted. He shot the bolt. As he did so a cold draught blew down upon his head. He looked upward, the fanlight over the middle window was open; the night wind, cool, moist, blew in from there. -

It's my house. You have no warrant, and I forbid you!" He had sunk back in his chair. Me could not have risen to save his life. The warder looked at his chief. Clark nodded.

The next moment the great iron door was thrown back. John Brandon could have screamed. Had there been a knife or a pistol handy he would have killed himself. Celia Verde would never, never be his now! _ _

Warder Clark stepped forward, then, as the door swung right back, he paused. "Empty," he said—" absolutely empty What "—he turned savagely to John Brandon—" have you made a fool of me for? There is nothing there." And he was perfectly right. Wherever he was, the dead man—the dead man who had been locked in the safe—was no lon,,er there. John Brandon pressed hands to his temples. A "CURRANT CHRISTMAS."

In view of recent developments, this new exp ression which appears in a recent number " of "The Grocer," will be readily understood by every housewife. There has never been a Ourrant Christmas before ! Last Christmas the Currant was beginning to be talked about, but its popularity then was nothing compared with what it is now. Neither is the present demand for the Currant confined any longer to Britgin. The IGerman house-mother—famed the world over for her insight and efficiency—is doubling her Currant order, and the housewives of Holland, Australia, and America are following along the same lines. Currant Bread is still increasingly popular with every class of society, while, as a proof of this enthusiasm we I may mention that many housewives have already secured from their own grocers, free copies of that most useful ittle book—" Currants : A Few Dainty Recipes." Ask your grocer for a copy to-day.

STOCKTON HEATH.

GARDEN SOCIETY.—This Society having obtained the kind permission of Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall for the members to view the chrysanthemums on Sunday afternoon last, at thr Conservatories at Walton Hall, a large number of the members and friends with many of tho public availed themselves of the opportunity of spending a pleasant half hour and enjoying the beautiful display, which, although past its best, was a very good show, and reflects great credit on the care and attention given to the plants by Mr. Goves, the head gardener.

POCKET PICKED OF £l,OOO.

The "Express " Paris correspondent sacs :—Mr. Motthity, a rich raineowner from Texas, had his pocket picked of £l,OOO on the Stock Exchange on Friday. He made the acquaintance of an Englishman on the voyage from New York, and the two men went to see the Stock Exchange together. Suddenly Mr. Motthuy missed the Englishman, and on feeling in his pocket found that a £l,OOO note had been abstracted and a note substituted, saying : " Forgive me, it was a matter of life and death." Mr. Motthuy has refused to lodge any complaint.

ARE SWEET THINGS WHOLESOME?

A Hint on Food for Children.

The idea that sweets and cakes are bad for children and growing boys and girls is one as general as it is erroneous. Some puddings and cakes as often made are undoubtedly bad, but where the right materials are used in making, they are a satisfying and satisfactory food. Meat is admittedly wrong food forchildren, who should depend for nutriment on natural wheat salts and sugars and starch. In what more palatable form can these be given than a nice cake. No nicer, more nourishing or wholesome cakes can be baked than those made with Paul's Cake Flour. This cake-maker is made from the purest materials perfectly proportioned and mixed. It is very easy to use and if the recipes given in Paul's newly published cookery book are followed, good results are certain. These recipes are all so clear and concise that you cannot go wrong. THE LATE COUNCILLOR BROM.

FUNERAL AT WARRINGTON,

The high esteem in which the late Councillor Walter Brough was held by all sections of the community was strikingly evidenced at the funeral which took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Monday. Representa- tives of public bodies and of all classes and shades of opinion attended to pay a last tribute to one whom they had been proud to claim as their friend. The cortege left the deceased's late residence, The Grange, Fearnhead, about noon, after a short service had been conducted in the house by the Rev. J. F. Matthews, and proceeded to Padgate Wesle)an Church where the first part of the burial service was held. A large congregation had assembled and the proceedings were most impressive. The Communion table and the pulpit were draped in blear. As the coffin was borne into the church, the organist (Mr. George Pendlebury) played " 0 Rest in the Lord "very feelingly. The opening sentences and the psalm were read by the Rev. W. Mellor (resident minister at Padgate), while the Rev. J. Manning read the lesson, and the Rev. Sidney Pitt offered prayer. The hymns" Lord it belongs not to my care "and " When the day of toil is done " were sung. _ _

The REV. JoBN GUNNELL delivered an address, in which he referred to the outstanding features of Coun- cillor Brough's career and character. He said that when quite a youth fifty years ago, Mr. Brough came to War- rington, and he ha A spent practically the whole of his life in the town. No-one was better known or more respected. For twenty-five years he was a prominent business man. The business which he had built up with his own skill and industry was successful, like most things to which Mr. Brough put his hand. From the year 1900 to the day of his death, he was a Town Councillor and occupied a prominent position on many of the principal committees ; so that he was diligent in ' business, and he was not unmindful of his duties as a citizen. In religion—and that was what they had on that occasion to do with mainly—he was a convinced and pronounced Methodist. The whole of his life was devoted to the interests of that Church, and he served It in almostevery capacity—lay preacher, trustee, steward, class leader, superintendent of the Sunday school, as well as being a very diligent worker /in the Band of Hope. He gave his services without stint for those many years, more particularly in connection with Sankey Bridges, and he was a very generous supporter of all their connexional and local funds. He (the speaker) did not want to convey the impression to any mind that Mr. Brough was a bigot—far from that. A man of broader views, kindlier charity, or deeper sympathies it would be hard, to find. Those whose knowledge of Mr. Brough was limited to the last three or four years could only form a very inadequate con- ception of the strenuous character of that life in its prime. Sixteen years ago and for ten or twelve years onward, Mr. Brough was the most diligent and tireless Christian worker he (the speaker) had ever met. During that time he was lay pastor of Sankey Bridges. There was no resident minister in that dense population and his services were in constant request in the'sick room. in the house of mourning,in the abode of poverthand by the graveside. Several very severe ,afilictions befel him during the past three or four years and his health was broken. His physical energy was very much reduced and while the spirit was willing to render such services as he had done in the stronger days of his life, the flesh was too weak to enable him to do so. Several times during the past day or two he (Mr. Gunnell) had heard the expression with reference to their departed brother " Only fifty-seven." They must not forget that life was not simply a thing of years. God counted our life not by time butgby service. Judged by the character built and the work done, and these were the true tests, Mr. Brough's life was not a short one. He "managed in those fifty years of his more active work to crowd in 'an amount of service that very few could hope to put into a life even of much longer space. During an intercourse with Ur. Brough stretching over seventeen years, he (Mr. Gunnell) had again and again been impressed with his thorough frankness and openness of character. His avowed intention was always his real intention—nothing was concealed. Duplicity or anything approaching it was foreign to his wholenatute. Then again there was his real love for work. To be moved by a high sense of duty in the activities in which one was engaged was a very good thing, and he was sure Mr. Brough was not wanting in that. But to be constrained by a mighty, quenchless love to go unto men was far better, and he was sure that that was the motive power of Mr. Brough's life —a great love for those whom he was endeavouring from day to day to render service. His work always seemed to be congenial to him, and this was especially so in his attempts, and they were many, to improve choir and congregational singing. To see Mr. Brough conducting a united choir at some great religious festi- val or Band of Hope " rally" was to impart a measure of his own enthusiasm to oneself. Those times were really a means of grace to Mr. Brough. He (the speaker) had seen him, when conducting one of those choirs, with his eyes filled with tears, and every feature of his face quivering with emotion, and there was no doubt he had a passionate love for that and every kind of wise and holy work. He (Mr. Gunnell) was also impressed with Mr. Brough's tender and tactful sympathy. It was shown in that beaming face of his —and the street always seemed to be brighter when one met Mr. Brough with that smile on his face. They heard it in the ring of his voice. Who that heard Mr. Brough laugh could ever forget it? He (Mr. Gunnell) was half afraid that we were forgetting bow to laugh and how to shake hands and Mr. Brough in those matters set them a good example. He was bright, generous, kindly and in all the varying conditions of life so good humoured. Of course he had his preferences and he would naturally be inclined to men of his own temperament, but he (Mr. Gunnell) very much questioned if his most intimate friend ever heard a word of disparagement or an ungenerous expression from his lips, touching any fellow-man. To put it all in one sentence he was a lovable man. There was something so winsome about his spirit and manner. Little children loved him : the poor loved him. Those in sickness and in sorrow held him in high and grateful esteem. He (Mr. Gunnell) would treasure that beauti- ful memory of Mr. Brough as one of the best possessions of his life. They prayed that the great Father, who was full of pity towaids all sorrow-stricken children of His, would comfort and sustain the bereaved family in their loss and grief, and also that the children might I follow Christ and work for the Church as their father did.

The service concluded with the "Dead March" in San!. .

The cortege then proceeded to the' Cemetery, being met at the borough boundary by members of the Cor- poration and other public bodies. There was a very large gathering at the Cemetery. The last rites were performed by the Rev. John Gunnell. Members of the deceased gentleman's Bible class acted as bearers, while a large contingent of members followed the hearse. The mourners and friends present included :—Messrs. Thomas, Walter. Harry and George Brough (sons), Mr. E. Wilkes and Mr. W. H. ONE OF MANY IN WARRINGTON. Whenever we are suffering from any trouble or ill- ness, it is surprising to find how many have suffered in the same way. Then the feeling of loneliness goes. If also we hear how they were able to overcome their trouble, there comes a sense of hopefulness, and we determine to follow the course which they pursued so successfully. This Warrington story will help many of us.

" I was so ill with kidney complaint for five years . that I didn't know what to do for the best," says Mrs. L. Selby, 52, Devonshire-place,Thelwall-lane,Latchford, Warrington. "I have many a time been doubled up with the pains, which were round my loins, and I did not know how to straighten myself. My head ached so badly at times that I could scarcely see. "The doctor, told me 'I was suffering from kidney disease, but I got no better under his treatment. "Hearing of • the good many people were deriving • from Doan's backache kidney pills, I got some to try. Now I can truthfully say tbat they have done me more good than anything else I ever used. I consider Doan's , pills a splendid medicine, and I am thankful for the • benefit I have received. (Signed). L. Selby." Of all chemists and stores, or post free, direct from Foster-McClellan Co., 8, Wells-street, - Oxford-street. London, W. Doan's are the pills that cured Mrs. Selby.

At the Manchester Assizes on Saturday, Oliver 1 Summers, boot dealer, formerly carrying on business in Bridge-street, Warrington, who the previous day had been found guilty of concealing from and failing to disclose and deliver up to the trustee of his estate, fo• the benefit of his creditors, 553 pairs of boots, shoes, . and slippers, of making material omissions in his state- , meat relating to his affairs in answer to his perliminary examination, and of mutilating and attempting to destroy documents relating to his property,w,s brought lup for sentence by Mr. Justice Sutton. 1 Mr. Gibbons, addressing the Judge in mitigation of sentence, said this was not a case in which the creditors had been robbed of any large sum of money. In fact, if they had lost anything it was a matter of teens of si pounds probably—not more than .£2O on the evidence : that had come out. He had had an opportunity a speaking to Summers, and had explained to him thit he 7* ought to do something for the benefit of creditors if 10, 1 could. It appeared that his mother-in-law had receiVr; in respect of her claim on the estate—which amounts(' A to £6oo—a sum of £l3O in dividend, and sumaers A I informed hins that his mother-in-law would hand back 4,1 that £l3O to the estate, so that in that way the eat J would not auffer at all through his action. With al regard to the mutilation of a document, it was a Vl, -,el • 4 clumsy matter, because when it was torn if, two it was discovered by the Official Receiver, and 139 bitn4 was done by it. With regard also to his book debts, : 'although hehad many month!. lii he could have Summers never attempted to collect one of them, , *done so if he had desired. Inspector Hough said he had made inquiries about Summers. He was a native of Warrington, and was I brought up to the boot and Shoe business. He had, before setting np in business for himself, occupied a • - position with a arm in the same trade in Manchester,. and in that capacity bad borne an excellent character. In Warrington there was nothingagainst his good record- - previous to this offence. Mr. Gibbons said Summers had asked him to explain further, that he had been somewhatimfortunate in the ' places in which he had traded in Warrington. On two occasions he had had to leave his businest premises in consequence of' street improvements, and bad in this way lost certain goodwill. Mr. Byrne, for the prosecution, said there was no desire to press the case against prisoner. At the same time the prosecution thought they should have some guarantee in relation to the sum of £l3O which it had been stated Summers had arranged to refund. The Judge suggested that in the circumstances now stated, it might be the better course to postpone sentence till the next Assizes.

Mr. Gibbons said Summers told him there need be no question about the guarantee. The money could be paid on Monday morning, or even on Sunday, if neces- sary. The Judge said that, on the whole, he would not take that matter into consideration. Addressing Summers, he said be had taken into consideration all that counsel had said in his behalf,and also what the police inspector had said. He sentenced the prisoner to four months' imprisonment in the second division.

Taxing the Piano.—The piano plays such an important part in the amusement of people during the wintei evenings, that dill proposal to tax it is not likely to Meet with much sympathy, even in France, where pretty I nearly everything is taxed. There is, of course, nothing ; new in the suggestion that pianos should contribute to the State, and such a step is being seriously considered in France. No doubt there are a great many people in this country who would not object to see.their neighbours pianos heavily taxed. To the dwellers in fiats, and some detached houses' the piano is capable of being made an awful nuisance, but they are in a minority, and upon the whole the amount of suffering which the piano inflicts is infinitesimal to the enjoyment that is got out of it. From the earliest Victorian days, it has become the proud and indispensable possession of every home that had a position to keep up, and the State dare not lay hands upon it. However this may, there is no doubt that,after the New Year tradespeople making gifts, or giving secret commissions to cooks, butlers, and other servants Will be liable to prosecution, as will also the recipients of such favours. Gas Cooking and, Health.—The Coal Smoke Abate - ment Society have been carrying out a series of experi- ments with gas cookers, and have come to the conclu- sion that the products of combustion are not, as is some- times supposed, harmful to food and health. Some people will not have gas cooking for this reason. They think that poisonous fumes are given off from the gas, -and that these contaminate the food, but if the gas stove has a proper flue the Society is satisfied that it is quite free from such objections, from a hygienic point of view. Of course everything depends upon manage- ment, and the great thing is, not to turn on more gas than is necessary, and never at such pressure that it passes through the burners unconsumed. One of the largest of the London gas companies send out women experts to gratuitously instruct customers upon the proper use of gas stoves. It seems at first sight singular that they should do so seeing that they only show people how to obtain the best results with the smallest possible consumption of gas, but that is the whole art of usinga gas cooker, and the company very creditably put the interests of its customers first. There are many things to be had in the way of peltry, says a Globe writer, which not only are as cosy, but also look as well—or almost as well—as their more costly prototypes. Particu- larly successful in this respect is the new sub- stitute for broadtail, pony skin. Pony skin dyed black has a lovely brilliant black hue, and wnen carefully treated the watering is as pretty as and more marked than in broadtail. The advantages of this substitute are twofold. It is vastly cheaper, and it wears twice as well. So soft is the skin of broadtail that it will hardly bear the stitches necessary to hold it together. Pony skin is hard, the hair is short and brilliant, and the effect styleful and pretty. It is mostly used for short and tightly-fitting coats. We saw one the other day which was a triumph in sartorial art. It was an absolutely plain, long-skirted, tightly- fitting coat like a man's morning Coat. It fitted to perfection, the watering marks were matched to ripple out from the, back seam, and seldom have we seen a perfectly plain coat that carried more style than it did. Much of the skin has to be sacrificed in order to get the water markings in the right place. Even so, the price is about a quarter what broadtail would be. Musquash dressed like sealskin is another very successful substitute, and very smart capes and Goats and boleros are made of it. Russian sable, ermine, and chinchilla, the leading furs of this melon, have not been subjected to the sincerest form of flattery with any degree of success. Imi- tations of these finest furs give themselves away without the saving clause of "a pound of tea"! PICTURE HATS AGAIN. Large velvet hats of the "picture" type, after two winters of neglect, have once more returned to fashionable favour. The -charming example pictured in our illustration is sketched from one . of the newest and most modish models freshly imported from Paris,-and is achieved in a beauti- ful shade of purple velvet. The wide and slightly bent brim is quite plain, but the crown is of the Tam-o'-Shanter shape, the velvet being ar- ranged in full rich folds over tba wire frame. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT NURSING ASSOCIATION.

Concert In Parr Hall.

It is gratifying to record that public apathy in regard to local concert enterprise gave place for once, on Monday evening, to something approaching adequate recognition. The concert so successfully carried out for the benefit of a deserving public institution will leave many pleasurable remembrances, as well, let us hope, as a substantial balance in cash in the hands of its self-sacrificing promoters. Looking at the announcements, one had a feeling that interest in the concert would largely centre round the re-appearance in Warrington of Miss Evangeline Florence with whom our townspeople have become happily familiar principally through the agency of the Musical Society. But the uniform excellence of much of the singing and playing falsified such expectations. Miss Florence's power of warbling with inimitable grace and finish the bird-songs of David or of vocalising the brilliant wattz-songs of some Italian composers; and her ability to delight' one with a trill graded into the most impalpable of pianissimos, are as unmistakable as ever. Yet this sort of thing has obviously its limitations, and often, as in this instance, it but serves as a foil in setting off the gifts of a restrained yet artistic contralto soloist such as Miss Esther Yunson proved herself to be in one or two quite sombre though soulfully rendered lyrical trifles. Mr. Enalyn Davies's singing impressed us as a distinct advance on what we remember to have heard on the last occasion when he graced the local concert-platform. A. finely resonant tone and an equally fine declamation marked his singing of both "Son of mine" and "The lowland sea "in which songs he may be said to have reached a culminating point of vocal effort. Mr. Bardsley's "Come into the garden Maud" and " Thora ' were quite refreshing in the sense that both these songs have about them a stamina and a trans- parency of purpose conspicuously and painfully lacking in much of that wilderness of lyrical striving after , something never attained which characterises so many of the songs of present-day composers, and of which the programme under notice contained several examples. And although Mr. Bardsley's fine tenor voice became occasionally irritatingly veiled at a critical moment—probably the result of cold—he yet infused into all that he did so high a degree of fervour and expressiveness as to quite capture his audience and bring down upon himself a storm of applause. Among the concerted vocal items there were some genuine tit-bits, in which category must be placed two at least of the clever quartets from Lane-Wilson's cycle, " Flora's holiday, the round, " Buy ! buy ! " deserving especial mention in this connection. Nor did the duet "Nearest and dearest" in which Miss Florence and Miss Yunson were associated prove less engaging nor less entirely successful in hitting the taste of an audience at once discriminating and enthusiastic. If the re-appearance at this concert of Miss Backsheen Wood after a considerable interval may be considered as marking her attainment of the maturity cf her powers as a violin soloist, it would seem that her playing embodies qualities which fall but little short of entitling her to front rank as an exponent of the instrument. In a number of pieces very diversified in subject and interest Miss Wood , demonstrated quite a remarkable facility of execution A MAGAZINE'S RESPONSIBILITY. coupled with a charming daintiness of style, and, The effect of a magazine article was illustrated in a while the tone she produced was now and hen not remarkable manner at the Lincoln Assizes on Monday, t when young labonrer entitled coins. altogether free from scratchiness, there was often a pleaded guilty to manufacturing magazine depth of feeling shewn in her playing which overlaid ‘article ee nk C o niinet e ;Tell! WOMEN IN BUSINESS.

- - It isn't any use in the world foi a woman in business to expect men in business to stand aside in any way, or to make special allowances for her because she is "only a woman," declares a writer in Smith's Weekly. They will compete with her on the basis of business, not on the • basis of sex. In social life they will be courteous and considerate to a woman. But in commerce they can't play the role of big protective brother to women in business. NURSING ASSOCIATION

ANNUAL MEETING. The Recent Resignation of Nurses. __,_

The annual meeting of the Warrington District Nursing Association was held on Tuesday evening in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street. Mr. F. Monks, presided over a small audience, which included Mrs. John Fairclough and Mr. J. Dutton Fairclough (secre- taries), the Revs. E. a E. Carleton, C. Harvey Cook and F. Vaughan ; Mr. John Fairclough, Councillor Burton, Mr. J. Rymer_ Young ,and Mr. George Walker.

===== 0002601_19061201_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
ABSENTEE.

Letters of regret at being unable to be present were received from the Rev. J. Yonge and the Rev. J. F Matthews. THE REPORTS. " SATIS7ACTORY WORK CONTINUED." In the ninth annual report the committee stated that the work continued to be very satisfactory. The thanks of the committee were due to all the subscribers and those public bodies whose tontributbns had made so welcome and substantial an addition to the funds. At the beginning of July, 1906, Miss Whitfield, the superintendent, and her two senior nurses, sent in their resignations, and early in .lily left the association. A new superintendent (Miss Reeve) had been engaged.and the committee thought she would give every satisfaction. A fourth nurse also left the town in order to take up work in India, so that the staff would be almost entirely a new one. To the Superintendent and the nurses the warmest thanks were due for their untiring devotion to their duties and the efficient manner in which they were discharged. The late Superintendent (Miss Whitfield) presented her report, in which she thanked Lady Greenall for her generous grants of groceries and clothing; also the Tramways Committee for their grant of free passes, and regretted the withdrawal of both. She thanked Mrs. Crosfield for her kindness in providing for the distribu- tion of groceries in cases of extreme poverty; Miss Darrah, who kindly gave £2 for the purchasing of luxuries for the most needy patients; Miss Naylor for her continued support of, and interest in, the invalid kitchen • Mrs. Lewis, who continued to cook the food free of charge; the authorities of the Technical Institute for supplies of food from the cookery classes for distribution amopg the very poor; Mr. Bellian, for bicycle repairs' ; Mr. R. Whitfield for joiner's work gratuitously done, and also to the anonymous donors of 3s. put through the letter box, and of £1 " for the use of sick and poor," sent by parcel post, together with a nightgown and pillowslip. The summary of cases is as follows :—Cases transferred from last year, 78; new cases, 591: visited once or twice but not entered, 151 ; or a total of 823 ; visits paid, 18,866. The TREASURER (Mr. Monks) submitted a statement of the financial position of the association which stated that it was better than the last, as the association had been able to pay off the debt on the nurses' home of £251 16s. 6d., but the income was still insufficient to meet the current expenses for the maintenance of six nurses. The loss on the year was £36 18s. 3d. after bringing in the balance of the amount collected for the debt on the home. The formation of a Workingmen's Committee in connection with the association had already proved a useful means of helping the finances, and he hoped would enable them.to clear off the exist- ing debt and enable them to add another nurse, bring- ing the staff to the required proportion of one nurse to every 10,000 to the population. THE CHAIRMAN'S_ ADDRESS. •

The CHAIRMAN moved the adoption to the reports He said with regard to the report of the general com- mittee, there was nothing special except with reference to the resignation of Miss Whitfield and some other nurses. He did not wish to say very much, but he thought it would be very unsatisfactory if the nurses after eight years of services did not win the hearts of the people among whom they waked. They had left with good feeling and the hearty good wishes of those they had left behind. At the sametime it did not do to think they were indispensible. Unfortunately two or three of their good subscribers had withdrawn subscriptions from something which had beed said to them in reference to the work of the Association. That could not be helped, and the committee' were conscious that they bad done everything possible and reasonable to make everyone connected with the Association comfortable, and to assist them to their fullest capacity in the work they had to:do. The work would go onjust the same,though perhaps not exactly in the same groove; at any rate it would be as efficient as ever and the subscriptions would be administered to the best advantage. Miss Whitfield's report showed that her work had been efficient up to the time of her leaving. They believed the new superintendent's work would be as efficient and well managed. (Hear, hear.) By the handsome donation of Dr. Markel, just before the close of the financial year, of £25, they had been fable to close with a balance of only £3B to the bad. A new committee had been formed, and they had an excellent example of what they intended to do by the concert given last evening, and he should be surprised if it did not leave a very satisfactory balance after all expenses had been paid. He hoped a more systematic and thorough canvassing and collecting of subscriptions in the works would prove to be a great help, and would do more than make up the deficiency and also any subscrip- tions they might have lost through the tittle-tattle of the last few months. There had been a rumour that the nurses had interfered in politics. Let him say once and for all that as far as he knew the association had nothing to do either with politics or religion. (Applause.) It was work quite outside those subjects, and he hoped it would continue to be outside, so that they could all act harmoniously together with one common object in view, namely the benefit of the poor. (Hear, hear, and applause.) Mr. GEORGE WALKER seconded the adoption of the report and said that the Workingmen's Committee were prepared to do all in their power to assist the Society. The Rev. C. HARVEY COOKE supported and said there were several things in the reports which had been submitted which were calculated to rouse a deeper interest in the association. For instance the medical men of the town had shown a larger personal interest in the association by sending at least 100 more cases than in 1905.(Hear,hear.)The beneficent effect of the work of the nurses in the homes was alluded to. In sick visit- ing very often amongst the poor to go into very stuffy rooms and inhale the atmosphere was a trying experi- ence for a sensitive stomach. One of the things the association was bringing about through its nurses was the accomplishment among housewives of cleaner rooms and fresher air, which was making sick visiting much more agreeable. (Hear, hear.) The result of the year's work had been admirable and useful and he was glad that the debt on the Nurses' Home had been paid off. (Applause.) Questions were asked with reference to the resigna- tion of the nurses, and it was pointed out that it would he better to give an explanation if possible. The CHAIRMAN said that whatever' action the late nurses took had been their own action and not at the request of anyone else. He did not think they did anything in favour of either the Liberal or Conservative Parliamentary candidate. If the nurses bad anything to complain of to a large extent it had been their own fault. The visiting was left to them, and there had been no interference so far as he knew on the part of the committee. If there had been any attempt on the part i of the staff to neglect the poor n favour of the rich committee would be the first to put an end to it when they heard of it. (Applause). Mr. I. RYMER YOUNG said he would like to point out the good work they had done among the poor, by the fact that there were 59-I new cases among them. There had, therefore, been no neglect of work on the part of the nurses. (Hear, hear.) Mrs. YONGH also spoke on behalf of the Ladies' Committee and said that the resignation of the nurses was their own act. The reports were adopted.

===== 0002601_19061201_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS TO THE STAFF.

'Mr. JOHN FA IRCLO UGH moved, and Councillor BURTON seconded a vote of thanks to Miss Reeves and the staff.. .

The CHArnmAti, in putting the resolution to the meeting, said he looked upon the association as an infirmary doing work outside. The Infirmary dealt with cases in the building. Of the two the Nursing Associa- tion was of more importance than the Infirmary,because it not only was a means of restoration to health, but taught the people to preserve their own health. (Hear, hoar and applause). The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to the Chairman proposed by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton.

A WARRI GTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Singular Proceeding in a Typhoid Case. A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday under the presi- dency of Mr. G. Pilling. There were also present : Mr* Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Dr.:llurray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, T. H. Ashcroft, G. Mather, W. C. Brooks, S. Davies, W. Flanagan, R. Bolton. T.Ptitchie, M. W. Seton, A. Brooks, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (Workhouse master). and A. Wright (superin- tendent of Padgate schools). SCARLET FEVER AT PADGATE SCHOOLS. At a meeting of the Padgate Schools Committee, the Medical Officer having reported the occurrence of two cases of scarlet fever and asked for instructions, it was agreed that he and the Superintendent be empowß-ed to take such action as they deemed best in the event of any extension of the outbreak. The question of the remuneration of Fred Yoang, acting as leading hand in painting the homes was considered and it was agreed that he be paid henceforth at the rate of B,ld. per hour. The minutes were approved. HOUSE COMMITTEE.

The House Committee resolved that Mrs. Atkinson, Miss Brown and Miss Brundrit be received as Brabazon ladies in place of Mrs. and Miss Johnson and Miss Roberts, resigned. SUBSCRIPTION TO THE N.S.P.C.C. A letter was read from Mr. Robert J. Pay, director of the N.S.P.C.C„ stating that last year he ventured to bring to the notice of the Board the work the Society was doing to enforce parental responsibility throughout I the country and to ask that in view of the very great value which the work had in the direction of relieving the rates, the Board would subscribe to the funds. His application was not successful, but in renewing it this year he would urge the Board to reconsider the matter, as the Boards of 353 Unions subscribed last year. The local Inspector (Mr. E. Cox) had instructions to assist the officers of the Board by making inquiries in any case they might report to him and by supplying them with any information from the extensive records in his possession as to applicants for relief, tramps, &c. Last year he dealt with cases involving the welfare of 1,058 children. The House Committee recommended the Board to give the letter sympathetic consideration. On the motion of Mr. MATHER, seconded by Mr. DA.VIES, and supported by Mrs. WmxissoN, it was decided to subscribe five guineas. STRANGE PROCEEDING IN A TYPHOW CASE. The Town Clerk of Warrington wrote stating that at a meeting of the Health Committee held last week, it was reported that the body of a person who had died of typhoid fever in the Workhouse had been allowed to be taken out of the Workhouse to the house of relatives. He was instructed to draw the attention of the Board to the serious consequences to public health which might ensue from such a proceeding on the part of the officers at the Workhouse. The committee further directed him to request that the Board would give such instructions as would prevent the repetition of such an notion as that complained of. In reply to the Chairman, Mr. AWBERY was under- stood to say that the facts were correct as far as [he knew.

Mr. ASHCROFT thought the matter a very serious one. -

The CHAIRMAN said improvements were contem- plated in the direction of providing another mortuary so they hoped there would be no similar occurrence. They were very sorry this case had happened. The matter was referred to the House Committee. ROMAN CATHOLIC RELIGIOUS INSTRUCTOR. A letter was read from the Local Government Board stating that remuneration of the Roman Catholic religious instructor at the Workhouse was not subject to the Board's sanction. FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN' "SENTIMENTAL NONSENSE." Mr. JOLLEY presented an interesting report on the Sth annual meeting of the Association of Poor Law Unions in Englani and Wales which the Clerk and him- self attended. It was, he said, the largest gathering of members yet held. As evidence of the importance of the Association's work it might be noted that during this year's Session of Parliament no less than 2G Bills affecting the powers and duties of Boards of Guars Hans had been introduced. In relation to these add to numerous administrative matters which had been dealt with by the Executive Council it was made clear that that body had accurately interpreted the wishes of the general body of the Asso- ciation, for though its action was criticised by a few melibers of rather extreme views, in every case its recommendations were approved by large majorities. There was considerable discussion upon the feeding of children attending elementary schools, a subject which had been referred to an Inter-departmental Committee and about which a great deal of sentimental nonsense was both talked and written. The suggestion that any large proportion of parents habitually sent their children to school unfed or underfed was an insult to the artisan and labouring classes. The evil, so far as it had any real existence, was almost infinitesimally small and could be quite adequately met by voluntary effort and the agency of the Poor Law. By an overwhelming vote the Association decided to support its Executive in resisting any attempts either by legislative or administrative action to violate the established principles of social and economic science. The cost of vaccination, the question of further legis- lation dealing with vagrancy, several technical matters relating to Assessment and valuation, and the present- tion of the views of the Association to the Royal Commission on the Poor Laws were dealt with. The really representative character of the Association appeared from the discussions, in which though the enthusiasm of energetic ignorance several times mani- fested itself rather noisily, the good sense of the majority ultimately prevailed. Mr. JOLLEY was thanked by the Chairman for his report. _ _

HARDIE a chance of railing against the Govern- ment in the House of Commons. Mr. Kitrit, Hearne never requires much encouragement. He is an avowed enemy of the Liberal party, though he so ofen finds himself compeiled to vote with it. Out of some words of Mr. Aseurrg he professed to extract a declaration that the Government does not intend to deal with the female suffrage question during the life of the present Parliament. There is no real ground for any such inference, or, we feel bonnd to admit,for inference to the contrary ; but if all other ques- tions were cleared out of the way, the woman suffrage question, we may point out, would not become urgent. It would be impossible to bring it forward in the present sessidn, how- ever urgent it might be, and no reason for dealing with it impetuously can possibly arise until - another General Election is approaching. It was stated on Tuesday that woman suffrage has 420 supporters in the House of Commons. These would be much more than sufficient to carry the measure desired. Would it not be wise for the women suffragists to permit their friends in Parlia- ment to do things in their own way and their own time? The fifty votes recorded on the side of the Opposition on Tuesday are certainly no formidable inducement to play into the hands of the Tory Iparty.

It will be a souroe of great £lO,OOO FOR THE gratification locally that the INFIRMARY, subscriptions towards the Infirmary Extension Fund have this week topped the £lO,OOO mark. Just over two-thirds of the required sum have now been subscribed, and building operations can justifiably be commenced forthwith. The response to the call on behalf of Warrington's noblest institution has been most generous and no one can doubt that the full amount of £15,000, large as it is, will be forthcoming in good time. The much-desired round sum of £lO,OOO has this week been reached by the aid of ample contributions from Mr. James Dodds and local companies with which he is closely identified—Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Ltd., and Parkinson's Ltd. The employes of the first- named CoFnpany thus make their sixth monthly instalment and have certainly done a great deal to demonstrate their indebtedness to the Infirmary.

We have before us a copy THE COUNCIL of the Town Council AGENDA, summons and agenda for next Tuesday's meeting. At the same time we are conscious of a distinct feeling of guilt due to the knowledge that we have no right to such a document. Has not the Council decided that the agenda—which contains all Committee minutes—shall be supplied to members and officials only ? The public which elects the members and pays the officials, and the Press which represents all the lot, have to know .nothing of what goes on except such as can be imperfectly gathered at a Council meeting. In the public interest we have waylaid and captured a stray agenda. In it we find a couple of special resolutions by Alderman Tinnion and Councillor Lord respectively, suggesting modifica- tions of the standing order relating to the publica- tion of the minutes and agenda:Alderman Tinnion's motion while it appears to restore the original states quo, omits all reference to the Press. We therefore cast it on oneside and turn to Councillor Lord's proposal,which seems comprehensive enough to suit even the fastidious taste of the public and ourselves. If carried—as we hope it will be— then the agenda, containing all minutes except those of a private nature, will be issued to the Council, the Press, the Property Owners' Associa- tion,the Trades and Labour Council,and any other public body. This is a substantially satisfactory proposition, though we think it is straining a point even in the proper direction of publicity, to supply copies of the minutes to other public bodies. There can be no harm in it, but it seems to us that publication in the Press would have ' amply sufficed. The main point is, however, that the veil of secrecy is to be once more torn aside and the light of day let into the doings of the Obuncil and its committees.

THE EXAMINER.

DECHIIIBBR lat 1906.

An unexpected breeze WOMAN AND suddenly rose up in the THE VOTE. House of Commons on Tuesday in connection with the demand for woman's suffrage. Lord ROBERT CECIL, who furnished the occasion, appears to be a fairly good substitute for his brother, Lord HUGH ; but there are some strong, and even strik- ing, differences of character. Lord ROBERT is most like his father, the late MAncitris or SALISBURY, actually was when Mr. DISRAELI called him "a master of gibes and flouts and sneers." He has two main characteeistics— he relies to a quite trying extent on his own presumption, which he supposes to be the presumption of others, that he is entirely above all meanness or subter- fuge; and he considers himself entitled, on the ground of what he believes to be his own entire openness and honesty, to insinuate with a certain wearying persistence that gentlemen on the other side of the House are constantly acting in oppo- sition to their convictions. Perhaps we ought to add, as a third characteristic, that he considers himself able to lay snares in the sight of the bird without the bird in question having the slightest idea that it is in peril. On Tuesday Lord ROBERT CECIL moved a new clause to the Plural Voting Bill. It was innocently described in the orders of the day as dealing with the date of operation of the' Act. As a matter of fact, it was expressed in these , terms :—" This Act shall come into operation on the first day of January after the next General Election, unless before that time an Act shall ' be passed giving to women the franchise on the same terms and conditions upon which it is enjoyed by men, in which case it shall conao into operation on the day fixed for the coming into operation of such last-mentioned Act." To:again make use of an ornithological illustration, this was an attempt to kill two birds with one stone. It would be very convenient indeed for the Conservative party if the Plural Voters Ac t assuming it to have been passed, were not to come into operation until after the next General Election. That would be as good as not passing it at all ; but, then, the proposed new clause also raised the woman's suffrage question. I Lord ROBERT CECIL was quick to see the political advantage of dragging this matter into the arena, just when the suffragettes are at the height of their fame and in the full vigour of their most demons- trative activity. Whether he is really in favour of woman suffrage, nobody knows. His cousin, Mr. BALFOUR, with his usual determination not to commit himself, said that he supposed that his hon. friend was in a way or in the main in favour of the enfranchisement of women. There seems to have been an instant and jeering and certainly appropriate cry of "Ishe ?" Really, nobody knows—apparently not even Mr. BALFOUR, who has long been a woman suffragist himself, and who ought to know. The incident is scarcely worth mention, except as an illustration of the somewhat despicable-little sticks by means of which the Opposition is play- ing the party game in the House of Commons. The object was, in the first place, to further inflame the suffragettes out of doors, and to give Mr. Kmn,

===== 0002601_19061201_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Jbcr- Auism the women to be one of aanizatione.. • General Elect - not in the

Both in point of mew, WOIVIRN LIBERALS. ship and enthusiasm al' Warrington W omen's Liberal Association can claim to be one of our most useful local party organizations. The fact that the long-prepared-for General Election is now laeenseevneendt tohf et ihnet epraesstt oh Ist hnot m ienm bt he rse ne a stth ed eggrreeaet ipnogl questions o nwsa s otfh teh me od saty 8. u eTe ehses faunl neural er mh ee el dt the migration from the Liberal Club to the more commodious Co-operative Hall for this occasion being fully justd by the largely increased attendance. 0.-casion was taken to deliver some excellent and encouraging speeches. Miss Oros- field,who presided,emphasized the fact that during the short time it has been in office the Govern- ment has succeeded in effecting several important progressive changes, and pointed out that it was ' hoped that several great Labour measures would very shortly be on the statute books of the country. She also drew attention to the experiments which are being made in regard to small holdings which should have considerable bearing on future action touching the land question. Miss K. ..4,,thworth Briggs—a young and attractive lady—delivered a most interesting address, her chief subject being Free Trade. Athough this was the great _issue at I the general election and everything was done to make the people understand its tremen- r dons import to the prosperity of the country, it is well that it should:not be allowed to drop. We have it from the advocates of tariff reform that notwith- standing the unmistakable verdict of the country in January, they intend to seize the earliest oppor- tunity of again bringing their proposals forward. It therefore behoves Free Traders to be fully prepared to defend the policy which lies at the base of our commercial success, and as women- -the chancellors of the home exchequer—would be the first to feel any reversion to Protection, they should be to the for when the time comes for another trial of strength.

The Army Council, acting TRADE AND Aums, in accordance with Mr. Haldane's promise to the, House of Commons earlier in the session, have now issued a set of instructions with reference to I the desirability of fitting the soldier while with the colours to find civil employment when he leaves the Army. Practically everyone is agreed that one of the chief drawbacks to recruiting, as far as the better class men of the community are concerned, is the fact that unless the soldier can attain high enough rank to earn a good pension he leaves the Army with but little or no pros- • " pect of spending his declining days in. com- fort. He is unfitted for civilian life by the conditions of service, he knows no trade, and even if he has been a clerk in the Army he finds it but little assistance to him, because the ranks of clerks in civil lire are already overcrowded, The Com- mittee which inquired into the question this year came to the conclusion that something definite might be and should be done to give the soldier a chance of acquiring a trade. There is no real difficulty about the matter, for, as the Committee pointed out, the fact that the Army carries out a great deal of work on its own behalf affords a ready-made school for the learners. Thus' such trades as carpentering, shoeing, and blacksmith's work can be taught in barracks ; railway 'Work is done by Erigineers, and might be taught by the practical tradesmen of the.; corps,eo also would be the case with electrical engineering. Saddlery and harness work, horse and motor driv- ing, butchery, and many other trades come into the daily life of the Army in these times, and there should be little difficulty in arranging for classes. Of course much depends upon the will of officers. The Army Council suggests that the first steps might be taken by a committ of the officers interested in technical inatruction ; ee but the ultimate success of the experiment will unquestionably rest with the regimental ofUcers,to

What funny, absent-minded, short-memoried people these politicians are ! Stockton Heath tree-planters were in high glee on Saturday—for was it not the anniversary of tree- planting day—a day, as Dr. Parker said, that will be for ever memorable in the history of the village , There was quite a surfeit of oratory, and more trees were planted with due ceremony. Judging from he tone of the speeches, all differences on the ques- tion appear to have been sink, and peace once more reigns in the land of the Heathens. . We believe the Board of Guardians will have the ' hearty support of the townspeople in their decision to subscribe to the funds of the N.S.P.C.C. In taking this course they are only following the lead of several hundred other Unions. As was pointed out, the pre- ventive work of the N.S.P.C.C. must in the long rim tend to relieve the call on the ratepayers, and there is no questioning the value to the community of this noble institution.

* * * Alderman Bolton, who made a great effort to retain his membership of the Watch Committee, and succeeded at the meeting on November oth, has since been appointed Deputy-Chairman to the Mayor. The Committee would be stronger without representatives of the brewing interest on its membership, and we hope that at the next appointment of Committees, the Council will thoroughly eliminate this undesirable element from the Watch Committee. We do not ques- tion the honour of the " trade " members in the least, but their presence in such a capacity is contrary to a widely held public opinion. *

Complaints were made to the District Council, and the head lamp- lighter was asked for an explanation. He replied that the almanac said there would be a full moon, and so be turned out the lights, according to instruc- tions. " But didn't you trust your common sense to tell you there was no moon ?" asked a councillor. "Nay," he said, "I trusted th'almanac ; I've 'ad it six year." He was made to produce it; it was an almanac for 1900! 04-

St. George's Mission.—Young Men's Bible Class. A very interesting presentation took place at St. George's Mission on Saturday last in connection with the Young Men's Bible Class when Mr. Kingdon, one of!the members, was the recipient of a very handsome silver cruet and timepiece on the occasion of his marriage. Mr. Turton, the leader of the class, in making the presentation referred in eulogistic terms to Mr. Kingdon and expressed the hope which he felt sure was shared by all the members, that he and his wife would long be spared to enjoy a happy and prosperous union. Liberal Soiree.—Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P , to

Attend.

We are informed that Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., will attend the Liberal soiree at the Lion Hotel on Thursday evening next, and deliver a short address. Owing to ill-health and the pressure of business the Member has not been seen in Warrinton since the General Election and his visit next Thursday will undoubtedly be the occasion for a warm welcome from many of his Liberal friends and supporters. Further particulars of the soiree, which will comprise both whist and dancing, are to be found in our advertising columns.

' Hospital Sunday. The annual meeting of the Hospital Sunday Com- mittee was held at the Infirmary yesterday afternoon. Mr. F. Monks presided and there were also present: the Revs. H. V.Pigot, E. C. E. Carleton, Cl. C. Briggs, W. Bracecatnp, John Yonge and C. Harvey Cook, Messrs. John Fairclough, A. J. Jolley (hon. sec..) and J. H. J. Hampson (secretary of the Infirmary).—The I hon. secretary reported that one place of worship I which contributed to the hospital in 1905 failed to do so in 1906 - Six commenced or renewed their contri- butions. On balance there was a net gain of £ll 7s. 2d. I under those heads. The total receipts had been £313 Bs. I increase over last year of £25 is. Of this amount £193 19s. Sd. was collected within and £ll9 Bs. sd. outside the borough. The expenses had been :—For advertising, £l2 14s. sd. ; for stationery and postage, £3 lie. 4d. ; and for special leaflets, of which 14,000 were distributed, £2 16s. 3d. ; a total of £l9 25., as compared with £lB 6s. 9d. last year.—The Chairman moved the adoption of the report.—The Rev. John Yong, in seconding, said it was the best report they had had for four years.—Mr. Jolley said that in point of the number of contributing churches,the report was the best they had ever had.—The report was adopted, —Mr. Jolley was re-elected hon. secretary.—lt was decided that the 10th March be observed as Hospital Sunday next year. - -

TABLE FLOOR, AND HANGING LAMPS in large variety. TABLE, patterns.—J. p. GARLAND, Iron- monger Bridge st., Warrington—Advt. Shorthand Writers' Association, Notwithstanding counter attractions there was a large number of members and friends present at the weekly meeting of this Association held at the Winmarleigh Cafe on Wednesday night last. As the Federation examination is to take place next week, the members took full advantage of the speed practice provided, some of the pieces read taxing the writers to their utmost. Illustrations on the blackb-mrd were given in phonography of unfamiliar outlines and useful nhrases. Mr. H. M. Ashton read an enjoyable paper entitled "A week in Paris." The lecture was delivered

in a delightful manner, a brief resume of a week's holiday in the "gay capital " beina, given, the principal places of interest and the beauti- ful sights to be seen being well portrayed. Interesting and humorous reference was made to some of the characteristics pecular to the French people, and also to the amusement which was caused by the Britisher who, on the slightest provocation, would talk, " French," many of the Lecturer's witticisms creating roars of laughter. It was generally conceded that the paper was one of the best which had so far been given to the Association. At the conclusion of the lecture, the Chairman (Mr. G-. J. Brown, F.1.P.5.,) in the course of a few remarks, mentioned that the Consultative Committee which had been sitting to consider the education question had decided that languages be not taught in our day schools, and it therefore devolved upon 111 would-be students of the French, or any other foreign language, to take full advantage of the courses offered by the v arious evening classes. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Ashton, which he suitably acknow- ledged.

STRANGE'S A.l. Crystal LAMP OIL in barrels or drums delivered free.--4 Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Methods of the Modern Press.

The methods of a certain section of the modern press were severely criticised at a meeting of the Warring- ton Society held at the Old Academy on Thursday. Mr. E. J. Hall presided over a large attendance.—The discussion was opened by Mr. A. E. Beck who gave an interesting resume of the growth of the periodical and daily press in England. Prior to 1870 he said, the press was not self-supporting and had to be assisted out of the pockets of private persons with thei result at that time the press was earnest and did not I pander to the public. The income from advertisements which had arisen since that time had a great deal to do with the decadence I of the Press. He charged the modern Press first with following and pandering instead of leading and educat- ing ; secondly, that it 'had chavul its idealism from literature to commercialism ; thirdly that it used its circulation to create an artificial demand for unneces- sary articles which it frequently vended ; fourthly, with sensationalism and fif thy with insincerity. —Mr. F. R. Roberts urged that when all was said and done a newspaper was a commercial enterprise. They could not get men to devote their lives to the pursuit of newspaper enterprise and at the same time demand that their fathers should leave them large fortunes in order that they might live, and therefore no one could object to the proprietor of a paper endeavouring to make his publication a commercial success. Mr. Roberts admitted the truth of the charges in respect to a certain section of the Press, but contended that there were many papers about which it could be said that scarcely one word of the charges was not an exaggeration.—On the motion of Mr. 0. Lofthouse, seconded by Alderman Bennett, I Mr. Beck was warmly thanked for introducing the I debate.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Death and Funeral of Mr. J. H. Halton.

Many of our readers will deeply regret to hear of the death of Mr. John H. Halton who passed aWay at the residence of his brother (Mr. Thomas Halton),Vine ' Cottage, Great Sankey, on Monday. The deceased, who was fifty-two years of age, had been unwell for some months and had been attended by Dr. Langdale. He first suffered from acute indigestion and although he was able to get out of doors until quite recently serious symptoms developed and he gradually sank Mr. Halton was a Warringtonian, having lived all hi • life at 73, Lovely-lane—the house previously occupied by his late father. He did not take any part in public affairs but was an active business man and was well known and respected by a wide circle. He had long I been identified with Holy Trinity Church and for many years discharged the duties of churchwarden, latterly acting as sidesman. He took the greatest interest in church work and his place will be difficult to fill. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Thursday afternoon. The first part of the burial service was held in Holy Trinity Church, the Revs. E. C. E. Carleton and H. R. Wild officiating. The hymn " Let saints on earth in concert sing" was sung and as the mourners left the church, Mr. W. B. Randall, who presided at the organ, played "0 Rest in the Lord." The last rites at the graveside were performed by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton. The chief mourners included: —Mr.T. Halton (brother). Masters Edward, Leslie and Cecil Halton (nephews), Mr. J. Street Halton, Southport (cousin), Messrs. R. Denham, J. Denham and A. Denham . (brothers-in-law),MissGarnett(Southport),Mrs.F.Hurter Mr.W. Leigh and Mr. J.Leigh. Amongst others present were : —The Rev. C. Morgan Price, Messrs. T. Welsby and W. Gill (churchwardens), J. Harding, R. Dobson, E. Rose, W. B. Randall, A. Walsh, E. Dyke and Dr. Braide. Wreaths were sent by the following: —Mr. and Mrs. T. Halton, " Eddie, Cecil and Leslie," Miss Ada Leigh, Mr. and Mrs. S. Street Halton, Rev. E. C. E. and Urs. Carleton, Holy Trinity. Young Men's Association, Mr. and Mrs. A. E. Denham, Mr. H. Denham, Mr. and Mrs. W. Leigh, Mr. and Mrs. J. D. Leigh, Mrs. Leigh and family, Mr. and Mrs. Finney and family, Mr. James Harding, Mr. and Mrs. E. Br aide, "Uncle Bould," Miss Garnett,Mrs. Ferdinand Hurter, Mrs. Percy Randall, Mr. and Mrs. Ferguson, Mrs. B. L. Pierpoint and family. The funeral arrangements were in to hands of Messrs. Maginn Brothers.
