RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

THE SPEED OF MOTOR CARS.

The Runcorn Rural District Council at their meeting on Tuesday, almost unanimously passed a resolution that the maximum speed limit for motor cars should be reduced from twenty miles to fifteen miles, and that causing excessive noise-vibration should be an offence ; that headlights with a maxiraum candle-power should be fixed; that owner as well as driver should be liable for punishment ; that taxation derived from motor-cars should be devoted to the improvement of roads; and that the scale of taxation in the Royal Commission report should be doubled. DEATH OF MR. T. WITEIINGTON, RUNCORN. Death has removed an old and respected Runcornian in the person of Thomas Withington, of 50, Ashbridge street. The deceased, who Nt as 70 years of age, had been in the employ of the Bridgewater Canal Under- taking as a boiler-maker for 40 years. He gave up work two years ago owing to ill-health, and had been ailing more or less ever since. He had to take to his bed six weeks ago. He was a member of the congregation of the Parish Church. He was associated with the Loyal "William IV." Lodge of the Order of Oddfello ws, and had been through the Past Grand degrees. The funeral 'takes place at Runcorn Cemetery on Wednesday. I TEMPERANCE DEMONSTRATION AT WIDNES. The annual temperance demonstration in connection with the Widnes Free Church Council was held on Sunday and Monday. On Sunday evening a well attended public meeting was held in the Victoria-road Chapel. Councillor D. Lewis presided, and addresses of a temperance charactbr were delivered by the Revs. A. Harvey and W. J. Baker and Mr. T. Dodd. On Mon- day evening a demonstration was held in the chapel. There was again a good attendance which was presided over by his Worship the Mayor (Alderman S. Owens). The Rev. James Watkins, of Liverpool, delivered an address entitled "Three hours in a London Inferno." The other speakers included the Revs. F. R. Bell and Joshua Pedlar. A satisfactory offertory was taken for the work of the Free Church Council. RUNCORN PRIEST'S ERROR. At the meeting of Runcorn Urban Education Com- mittee, on Wednesday, correspondence was submitted in which the Rev. Father Donlevy, of St. Edward's Roman Catholic Church, writing on the 14th inst., intimated that until the end of the week St. Edward's Schools would commce secular instruction at 9 a.m. land finish at 11.30. A long discussion took place on the priest's action in withdrawing Catholic children at 11.30 to take part in a mission. Father Donlevy said that he had taken the action he did in ignorance of the fact that it was against the rules of the authority. Ultimately it was decided that copies of the log-book be forwarded to the Director of Education, together with the opinion of the committee that, having heard Father Donlevy's explanation, they believed it was a departure from the .by-laws through ignorance, and were satisfied that such an irregularity would not occur again. FREE SPEECH AT WIDNES. SALVATION ARMY DErONSTRATION. RELEASED PSISONERS WELCOMED. The five Widnes Salvationist officers, Ensign Catherine Martin, Lieutenant Lazelle, Mrs. Wynn, Miss Kershaw, and Mr. Arthur Cavvsey, who twenty-one days ago were incarcerated in Walton Gaol for the non-pay- ment of fines inflicted for obstructing the free passage of Victoria-square, while holding their Saturday even- ing services, went home to Widnes at 7.48 on Tuesday night. The demonstration held in the Widnes Theatre on November 10th almost set the town enthusiastic with sympathy, and the huge crowd which congregated at the London and North-Western Railway Station on Tuesday evening bore evidence of thestrong resentment towards police interference at the Big Lamp. The Warrington and St. Helens Bands played selections while waiting to welcome the train. To the Drill Hall those who had been imprisoned were conveyed in a landau, dressed in prison uniform. The spacious hall was packed to suffocation, and fully 2,000 were turned away. The presence of the five "convicts" on the platform created a stirring scene and a violent outburst of enthusiasm.- -Salvation Army Colonel Wilson, commander of the North-West Province, took the chair, and numerous letters of sympathy from leading public men up and down the country.—The Rev. A. Harvey said it was not usual for prisoners returning from gaol to be received in such a manner as that. They had not only received a town's welcome, but a civio one. Their five friends had made a noble stand in fighting the people's claim in Widnes, to meet in public orderly assembly in the only available spot worthy of the town. The question at issue was so vital for the whole community that if the Salvation Army retired from the fight others would have to step into the breach and carry on the conflict One shilling and costs, or ten shillings ani costs, did not enter into the question. How could the ' police retreat when the magistrates were show- ing the iron hand? The Homo Secretary must be approached in terms that would be 'unmistakable, for the magistrates upon the bench of I that borough were responsible, and not the Salvation Army, for the unrest that had been created. It was said that the army were obstinate in not accepting the site offered them. They had never been offered a site. Why ; should they ask for a site, when they were perfectly satisfied with the one they had got ?—Lieutenant Lazelle I (a lady) thanked them heartily for the splendid welcome I I they had received. Their suffering had been joy to I them, providing freedom could be gained.—Mrs. Wynn I also spoke.—Ensign Martin and Mr. Arthur Cawse briefly narrated their prison experiences, and they were ready at any moment to go to do duty at the Big Lamp. Commissioner Nicol, who was loudly cheered, said he had been sent from London by his chief, Mr. Bramwell Booth, to convey his warm thanks to the comrades in Widnes for the admirable stand they had made for the maintenance of the moral right to stand in that part of the town to preach the Gospel of Jesus Christ. He did not pretend to knowthe full circumstance which led up to the prosecution, but he was glad to find a great body of the public in sympathy with their aim, and he would say emphatically that every man and woman looked upon the action of the police in Widnes as hipocrisy The authorities had displayed a great amount of tactlessness; they had rushed blindly, and did not fore- see how far they would eventually drift. He was anxious for the reputation of Widnes that good order would prevail, and that the quarrel would soon fizzle out—a quirrel for which the Salvation Army was in no way responsible.—The meeting, which was very enthusiastic throughout, was interspersed with hymns in which the whole of the audience joined.

WIDNES POLICE AND SALVATIONISTS. Proceedings at the Salvationists' meeting in Victoria- square, on Saturday evening,were somewhat uneventful. At the usual hour it was evident that the increased fines imposed at the last Petty Sessions were not acting as a deterrent, for ten officers arrived and commenced the usual service. Inspector Blackburn° and a couple of sergeants arrived on the scene soon afterwards, and the names of the following ten officers were taken : Lieutenant Fisher, Birkenhead ; Lieutenant Atkinson, Sutton Oak ; Captain Meredith, Everton ; Ensin Spicer, Warrington; Captain Clarke, Liverpool; Captain Downs, Warrington ' • Lieutenant Winstanley, Litherland ; and LieutenantTupholme,l Thatto Heath. The hymn, " Rock of ages," was sung during the taking of the names. There was no demonstration whatever against the police. Colonel Wilson in the course of an interview, said that the Chief Constable of Lancashire had visited Widnes during the last week and examined the Square. He hinted that the result might be in favour of the " Army."—A good crowd was again present at the service, and the collection appeared to be very satisfactory. EIGHTEEN SALVATIONISTS IN THE POLICE COURT. Widnes Police Court was crowded to suffocation on Thursday when eighteen members of the Salvation Army were summoned for obstructing Victoria-square by holding an open-air meeting. They pleaded,as usual, , not guilty.—lnspector Blackburn and Sergts. White- head and Blackball gave evidence as to obstruction having been caused on the 10th by a crowd of from 1,000 to 1,500 people.—The officers in cross- examination admitted that but for the notoriety brought about by the prosecutions there would have been comparatively no crowd at all.— A number of the Salvation Army officers gave evidence, , declaring that no obstruction had occurred. Major Cunningham said an omnibus and three horses had been driven round the Square and round the Army ring when the meeting was in progress.—Mr.Jager, barrister, of Liverpool, urged that no real obstruction had ever been caused. The evidence was simply that of police- men sent out to look for obstruction.—The Mayor ex- pressed sympathy with the Salvationists, but said the Bench were bound to convict, as obstruction had been proved. They would be fined each 10s. and costs, or 1 seven days.—The Bench dismissed a similar case against Rosa Boorman, a pretty Salvationist lassie, charged in respect of October 27th. ROMANTIC STORY 1N THE BANKRUPTCY

COURT.

At the Liverpool Bankruptcy Court,on Monday,before Mr. Registrar Cooper,Marcus John Eustance,a physician and surgeon who had practised in Widnes, came up for public examination. The statement of affairs shows liabilities of £l5l and net assets of £B9. The bank- rupt related a remarkable history.—An Irishman by birth, he qualified in Dublin and with money received from his .father's estate purchased a practice in Alresford, Hampshire, for £l,OOO. This he retained for a few years, and then sold it for £1,200. He then went to Persia under the Church Missionary Society, and then left that society for the next three years for the purpose of becoming port medical officer and surgeon to tbe British consulate under the Indian Government in the Persian Gulf. Then the Church Missionary Society wanted him back again, so he went to Quetta, Baluchistan, and remained there about seven years. He then come home and went out to East Africa for the Church Missionary Society for six months to stop a smallpox epidemic. He then came home again and went to South Africa.— When the war in that country had concluded he came home, and went out again for the Colonial Office up the Niger. He was next invalided home, and, said the bankrupt, "I was pretty well on my beam ends when I came back." He returned in the summer of 1903 and went to Widnes, where he obtained employment in a foundry as a labourer.—The Official Receiver: Why

did you not go on with your profession ?—Because I was quite broken down in health. I was suffering from fever badly. I was unfit to work, and had no money to start with.—And you could not get anything but labour- ing ?—I thought myself that labouring was the best thing I could go on with for a time—Why ?—I was not nervously or physically fit to do any work. No doctor would take me as an assistant. I had ague three times a week regularly.—The next questions of the Official Receiver revealed a romantic story. An acci- dent had happened at the works where the bankrupt was employed by which a man's arm was broken. The bank- rupt came forward and set the limb in such a style that when the man was taken to the hospital it was discovered that a practised hand had been at work.— Inquiries were made, with the result that the bankrupt who had worked at the foundry under an assumed name revealed himself as a qualified doctor. He then started practice in a small house at Ss. a week, and after a time removed into a better house, and declared that had it not been that a firm of wholesale chemists from whom he ot his drugs had put him in the Bankruptcy Court, he would, have been able to establish himself. —Elections of surgeons to works at Widnes took place annually. He had on two occa- sions lost an appointment of this kind by one vote only, and he believed that had the pressure by the wholesale firm in question not been applied he would have gone on, and would have received an appointment of the kind named, and would then have had no occasion to go into the Bankruptcy Court.—The bankrupt under- took to supply the Official Receiver with information as to the value of book debts, which he has returned as owing to him. On this undertaking the Official Receiver assented to the closing of the public examination.

ST HELENS.

_CYCLING OUTRAGE NEAR ST. HELENS,

I Miss Jennie Lathom, aged sixteen, who lives at Mill Green, Bold, when cycling along Mill-lane, near an old quarry between St. Helens and Farnworth, on Monday, was attacked by a tramp who knocked her off her bicycle and demanded money. The man noticed that the girl had a watch strapped in a bracelet on her wrist. He demanded this and Miss Lathom told him that if he would let her go she would give him it. She also gave him all the money she had,but the man struck her several times on the head with a stick. She screamed, and on the man noticing that a man was ploughing in a field close by he left Miss Lathom, who was terribly Flistressed and frightened. She made her way home as well as she could and information was given to the police. The man was originally going 1 towards Widnes, but he afterwards went across the fields towards Rainhill.

SOCIALIST M.P.'S AT ST. HELENS. ADDRESSES BY MR. J. A. SEDDON AND MR. ROSS CLYNES. HUMANISED POLITICS. The St. Helens Socialist Society on Sunday afternoon held a large demonstration at the Hippodrome, Mr. P. Pugh presiding. The Chairman said that what they had to do to-day was largely to prepare the people for the great coming advent of Socialism. Mr. Forsitsw moved a lengthy resolution, expressing satizfaction at the rapid adoption of Socialistic principles in municipal as well as in national legislature, and approving of the vigorous efforts of the Socialistic party to establish among the people a true ideal of civic life, including security of livelihood, for all which the inequitable industrial conditions at present existing could not provide. —Mr. J. Francis seconded.—Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., supported. He said that individualism was the political gospel of both Liberal and Tory. While they might differ on the platform, at the bottom and base of their political organisations both parties were identical and the same. The social conditions of the country were the result of the workings of centuries, but to-day they had reached the parting of the uays. Every man who had any sympathy for the outworn veteran, for the under-fed school children, for the unemployed men must face tee subject, and inquire whether it was no, possible to formulate a higher philosophy that would bring them nearer to the golden age of the brotherhood of man. (Applause.) It was an absolute falsehood, based upon wilful misrepresentation, to say that Social- ists were Atheists. While Socialism was not a religion, it was nearer to the religion of the Man of Nazareth than any other political faith. Socialism was based on the code of ethics that was common and is still common to Christianity. Mr. Seddon also spoke on the subject of feeding school children, and on the condition of the aged and wornout workers, and the attitude of the present Government towards these questions.— Mr. J. R. Clynes, M.P. of Manchester, also defended the Socialist cause, and said that it was untrue that the Labour party was a class party. Their efforts were directed to the end of obtaining legislative and indus- trial changes to uplift and improve the people as a whole. The political thought of the country was awakened to the justice of their demands, and the Socialists and Labour men were now a great and most important factor in the politics of the country. Referring to the Chinese labour problem, the Prime Minister had been reduced to that state that he said he would leave the question of repattiation of the Chinese to the new Government of South Africa. That meant that the home Government, which permitted Chinese labour to be introduced into South Africa, was now shirking the opposite duty of determining finally and positively that Chinese labour should, end, and were throwing the responsibility upon the shoulders of the colony.—The resolution was carried, after a very quiet and orderly meeting.

LIBERAL CLUB,

Distribution of Bowling Prizes.

One of the most enjoyable concerts ever held at the Liberal Club took place on Thursday evening on the occasion of the distribution of the prizes gained in the bowling competition decided during the summer months. Great interest is taken in this healthful sport by the members of the club, and the games were very keenly contested. The President (Hr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P.) has always been to the fore in encouraging bowls, and this year he again pre- sented a handsome collection of prizes. It was a matter of regret that owing to Parliamentary duties he could not attend to hand them to the winners. The concert, which had been arranged by Mr. E. A. Sanders, was a complete success. There was a large 1 attendance, ladies being invited. Alderman Tianion presided and was supported by Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. J. E. Warren, James Critchley, J. Worrall, W. Bibby, T. Pemberton (secretary of the club) and others.

An admirable programme was submitted and was highly appreciated. Miss Woodland (soprano), vary sweetly sang " When the heart is young" and was warmly applauded, and later in the even- ing she was heard to equal advantage in another song. Miss Lily Fairhurst possesses a rich contralto voice and she gave inter- pretations of " Kathleen Mavourneen " and "Come back to me"—the latter with its subdued chorus being specially effective. In response to an encore she sang the ever-popular "Killarney." Mr. J. Willett is a very acceptable tenor and he expressively sang " Star of Bethlehem " " An even' song" and as an encore piece, "Good-bye dearest." Mr. P. J. George (baritone) was in excellent voice and gave a fine rendering of "The sailor's grave." Two very pleasing duets were "The elfin call " by Misses Woodland and Fairhurst and "The parting hour " by Miss Fairhurst and Mr.., C. Fairhurst. Concertina solos by Mr. T. Hardman proved popular. The Lyric quartette party (Misses Woodland and Fairhurst, Messrs. R. W. Cook and P. 1 I. J. George) scored a great success in the laughable item " Toothache." Clever conjuring by' Mr. P. Bradford mystified the audience. Mr. E. A. Sanders ' quite maintained his reputation as a humorist,his lecture on" Physiognomy "and recital of the events attendant upon the presentation of "The Liverwing testimonial" creating great mirth. Mr. J. H. Foster acted as accompanist. Before distributing the prizes Alderman TissioN expressed regret that the donor (Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P.) was unavoidably prevented from being present. His Parliamentary duties compelled him to be in London and much as he would have wished to be present to meet his friends of the Liberal Club and present those hand- some prizes, he must bow to the inevitable, and he (Alderman Tinnion) could only express his regret,which be felt sure was shared by everyone present, that the Member was unable to be with them.

Aid. TENSION then presented the prizes to the following: —l, J. Davies, case of cutlery ; 2, M. Tobin, walnut bracket clock • 3 1. Jennings, one dozen table knives and forks ; 4, W. H. Dutton, clock ; 5, J. Smith, cruet ; 6, W. Daintith, salad bowl and servers ; 7, W. Warbur- ton, shaving stand ; 8, R. Fogg, case of carvers ; 9, C. Hibbert, pair of salts in case ; 10, T. Pemberton,pair of gold links ; 11, W. Patrick, pair of cut glass and silver salts • 12, E. J. Mitchell ; 13' J. Welsby, sugar dredger ; 14, T. Cooper, silver-plated butter dish ; 15 G. Vernon, dressing case; 16, J. Wareing, jam dish. Mr. J. E. WARREN moved a vote of thanks to Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., for presenting the prizes. He said the bowling green had been continuously improving, both as regarded the green itself and the number of members. In 1904 they had 45 members ; in 1905 the number had increased to GO, and in 1903 it stood at 90. (Applause). In the competition for which prizes had been distributed that night, they had seventy-two contestants, and it was fought very keenly. While the improvement of the green had a great deal to do with the success achieved, that success was in no small measure due to the interest Mr. Crosfleld took in it and the valuable prizes which he presented. (Applause). Alderman TISNION said Mr. Crosfield not only took a deep interest in that club but in everything that was for the improvement and enlightment of the town. He was especially intere3ted in the anchinery of the Liberal party and he was a particularly keen supporter of the Liberal Club and of bowling. He (Alderman Tinnion) did not hesitate to say that no one in War- rington had done as much as Mr. Crosfteld to foster and help forward tho grand old game of bowls. It was essentially the workingman's game and it brought out some of the finest qualities a man possessed, as it required patience, skill, tact and judgment. The vote was passed with enthusiasm. A vote of thanks was accorded to the artistes on the proposition of Mr. WORRALL.

TERRIBLE FATALITY NEAR WARRINGTON.

-- Two Men Drowned In the Ship Canal.

A terrible accident occurred at Acton Grange near Warrington, yesterday. For some time men have been at work painting the lofty railway bridge uhich spans the Manchester Ship Canal and to do this have ! had to operate from cradles slung underneath the structure. The cradles were left suspended when operations ceased on Thursday night and when work was resumed yesterday were apparently perfectly safe, and the men had no hesitation in trusting themselves upon the appliances. Work had been carried on for a shoit time when one end of one of the cradles became detached and swung downwards. Five men were working upon it at the time and almost before they had realized what had happened three of them were precipitated a distance of seventy-five feet into the canal. The other two men succeeded in clinging to the secure end of the cradle and their cries for assistance quickly apprised some men who were working near the scene of the accident, and help was at once forthcoming. Ropes were passed between the girders of the bridge to the two men, and by this means they were extricated from their dangerous position. The three men in the water were seen to be struggling but only one could be reached, the rescuers being forced to stand idly on the canal side and watch the other two men sink. One of these, Walter Reid, Nantwich, was a good swimmer, but he had apparently been partially stunned by the shock and was unable to help himself. Grappling operations were at once commenced and the body of George Pickford, 14, Peel-street, Crewe, was recovered almost immediately but artificial respiration was unavailing. Late yesterday afternoon the bady of Reid had not been recovered. John Davies, Broad-lane, Nantwich, one of the three who fell into the canal was considerably bmised and shaken by the accident but was otherwise unhurt. The two men who clang to the cradle were Thomas Sewell, Con-fields, Nantwich, and Arthur Sbakespear, Wrexham. The cause of the accident is unknown but it is not improbable that the high wind which prevailed was a largely contributing factor. A particularly distressing feature, we understand, was that Reid was to have been married shortly. Later in the day a large crowd assembled to watch the grappling operations which were continued by the Runcorn Grappling Corps under Mr.Byfield.

CALEDONIAN ASSOCIATION. -- St. Andrew's Day.

Yesterday, loyal Scots throughout the world cele- brated the feast of St. Andrew, their patron saint. Nowhere is the festival more consistently observed than in Warrington and the proceedings at the eighteenth annual dinner of the Warrington Caledonian Association last night were marked with the greatest enthusiasm. Favourite Scottish dishes were partaken of and toasts honoured in true Highland fashion, whilst national and patriotic emblems were freely displayed. The dining room at the Lion Hotel had been specially decorated for the occasion and artistically draped behind the presidential chair, the Scottish Royal ensign and the blue and white banner of St. Andrew formed a fitting centre piece. The popular president of the Association Mr. James Monro, to whose enthusiasm the body owes ; much, presided whilst the vice-chairs were occupied by ; Mr. James Skinner, and Mr. J. A. McGregor. Local Scots and their friends, to the number of 70 were also present. An excellent dinner was served by Mr. John Lee, after which the evening was spent in speech making 1 and sohg. The Royal toasts including "The Duke and , Duchess of Rothesay" were honoured on the opposition of the president. The toast of the evening" Scotland for ever" was entrusted to Mr. A. E. Beck who did it full justice. Other toast were "The Navy, Army and reserve forces," proposed by Mr. W. Skinner and res- ponded to by Colonel Edelsten ; "Corporation and Trade of Warrington," proposed by Captain Crostield and responded to by the Mayor ; "Our Patron "pro- posed by Councillor Fulton Smith, and" Our Guests " and "The President," proposed by Alderman Fors)?aw and Mr. G. Bowman. During the evening musical items were given with success by Messrs. J. L. Daven- port, T. D. Amos, F. Whitehouse, J. B. Poole, and J. A. Westcott.

ENGLISH CHURCH UNION.

Rev.. John Wakeford on "Dangers and

Necessities." "

THE EDUCATION QUESTION.

IThe annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the English Church Union was held at the Masonic Hall,. Bold-street, on Thursday afternoon, under the presidency of Mr. John White. There was a small attendance including the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), Rev. IL A. Lester, and Mr. C. S. Burgess (secretary and treasurer.), The Rev. John Wakeford, vicar of St. Margaret's, Anfield, was the principal speaker. Tha following officers were elected :—Chairman, Mr. John White; vice-chairman, Rev. Wilfred Stanton ; secretary and treasurer, Mr. C. S. Burgess ; committee, Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth, Rev.J. Holden (Antrobus), Mr. G. Egerton Warburton and Mr. H. N. Houghton. The CHAIRMAN, alluding to the report of the Royal Commission on Church discipline, said he supposed it was called together with the intention of cursing the High Church party, but instead it had to a certain extent blessed them. The Commission practically said: "We do not think the Ritualists have done what we approve of, but at the same time they have been more or less justified by the Ornaments Rubric." He thought the matter could be safely left in the hands of Convo- cation and that nothing would arise in any way to interfere with the work of the Church, but on the , contrary good might come of the discussion. He I did not, however, think there would be found in r Convocation any body of men who would be tempted to alter the Orpaments Rubric or the Prayer Book. With regard to Education, the spirit of compromise was abroad. There was nothing to say against compromise as arule, but he felt that in questions concerning the Church when compromise was in the air it was always the Church that had to give way while the other side gave up nothing. He hoped, however, that the protest meetings which had been held would have such weight with the House of Lords that they would not give way further than they had done already. All these attacks on the Prayer Book or on the schools were only part of an organized attempt to reduce the Catholic Church of England to the level of the Protestant sects, and against that it would be the aim of the Union to fight to the bitter end to defend the rights of the Church. (Applause.) The Rev. J. WAKEPORD took for his subject "Dangers and necessities." One danger which he emphasised was that of Erastianisrn. They were, he said, in danger of supposing that in some way the State had a right to interfere in the government of the Church. The supremacy of the Crown was sever the government of the Church by the Crown and whatever rights the Crown ha d,had never been transferred to the House of Commons. lithe House of Commons had any rights in the govern- ment of religion, there were reciprocal obligations with regard to the House of Commons ; it must be composed of Church people. Since they did not pretend that that was the case, it was positively indecent for the House of Commons, released from all obligations of religion, to profess to govern them in the matter of religion. The danger of Erastianism was that if the rulers of the Church were not sufficiently alert, they would get the word, the command and the direction from Parliament and the King's Courts. Churchpeople were not to allow that even now, when *they were threatened with some re-shaping of the Prayer Book—which he could not believe would come about. They were not to allow that any of these changes were to be made from without. They heard of compromise in regard to education. All their difficulty had come from the compromise they were led into in ISt). They ought never to have allowed the setting up of that new-fangled religion in the Board schools but to have boldly claimed that there should be teaching in all public elementary schools of religion as it might be acceptable to the parents. (Applause.) He (Mr. Wake- ford) had always been a Liberal, and he had read the Prime Minister's manifesto with interest. He (the Prime Minister) knew he was talking nonsense ; he had been appealed to a thousand times in private and told that the thing would not do ; he was as well aware as any of them that the Bill was not going to pass in any shape at all ; and he was not unwilling that it should be checked and thrown back by the House of Lords. Mr. Wakeford dealt with the danger of lawlessness arising from State interference, and also empha- sized the duty of teaching the Faith before press- ing matters of ceremonial. He rEgretted that at the Barrow Church Congress it was stated by one speaker that the principles of the Reformation were wholely wrong and that they had only to regret the Reformation right through. He (the speaker) did not regret the Reformation and if a second Reformation of a worse character came they could only attribute it to those persons who tallied so foolishly and acted so wildly. The Reformation was a necessity of the 16th century and they might thank God that it did not leave the Church worse off than it did. They had maintained the Apostolic succession, the Sacraments and the faith of the Primitive Church. (Applause). _

. „ The RECTOR, in movi„ng a vote of thanks to the Rev. J. Wakeford,said things which had been said very extrava- gantly by some people lately had prejudiced the great cause which they had at heart. They had no desire to copy Rome. They had no sympathy with the doctrines and practices distinctive of Rome, and they had no sympathy with those who differed from them on the other They wanted to make it felt that the Church of England was not a Protestant sect, but that it was the Catholic Church in this country. (Applause.) Mr. BURGESS seconded, and the vote was heartily accorded.

Stack Fire at Latchford.

Last evening the Warrington Fire Brigade were, called out to a fire near Latchford Locks. On arrival they found that two stacks belonging to Mr. T. Ashley, farmer, were well alight. There was a strong wind, and despite the efforts of the brigade the stacks were completely destroyed. The damage is estimated at.. M5O.

ALLEGED ASSAULT WITH A GUN. CHARGE AGAINST A BROTHER-IN-LAW.

At the Warrington Occasional Conrt on \Vednesday, Samuel Kelsall, moulder, Clay-lane, Bartonwood, was charged with unlawfully wounding his brother-in-law, James Haverty, a collier. It was stated by the com- plainant that on Wednesday night last week he met the prisoner in Clay-lane as he was returning trom a visit to his father, who lived next door to Kelsall. After using threats, prisoner went into his house and returned with a double-barrelled gun, exclaiming that he would blow the plaintiff's brains out. Whilst turning round to return home, Kelsall struck him a violent blow on the forehead with the butt-end of the gun, inflicting a severe wound, which rendered him unconscious —Sergeant Bannistsr, hearing a disturbance in the neighbourhood, went to the place and found Haverty in a dazed condition, and bleeding from a wound in the head. When arrested in his house prisoner made a remark that Haverty must think himself lucky he had not shot him," and concluded that he would do the same again. On examining the gun, the stock was found to have been broken, end the blow must have been given with considerable force.—ln reply to Mr. Knowles, who defended, complainant denied that he was in the Elm Tree Inn on the night in question, and also denied that he followed the prisoner on his way home.—Subse- quently the chlrge of wounding was withdrawn, and Kelsall was remanded to the Newton Petty Sessions.

OLD RUNCORNIAN FOUND DROWNED.

- - - - On Wednesday the body of Tames Littlemore, of Vine-street, Runcorn, was taken from the Weaver Canal at Weston. The deceased, who was seventv-Sis years of age, left borne in the morning to go or a walk, ani as he had a cataract in each eye and his sight very defective, it is supposed he strayed into the canal. Mr. Littlemore had been employed at Runcorn docks about half a century.

Advice free by letter, following the lead of similar associations, the share Loans will be accepted at about 4 per cent. If practicable the co-operative element will be intro- duced, so as to enable the tenants to share in the sucgess and management of the Company. Slould the scheme meet with the support of the people of Warrington it is hoped that capable business men will be associated with the management, as we deem this essential to its success. The Company will be run on business lines. The only place where philanthropy will come in will be in the shareholders agreeing to take not more than 5 per cent. for their investments. We do not consider that there will be any appreciable risk of loss of any capital. This will be invested in land and houses which cannot run away. "The Ealing Tenants' Limited" have been able to pay the full 5 per cent, on the capital from the second year of their existence, and onwards. If the Warrington Company should prove equally successful, the shareholders will have little reason to complain of the return upon their investment. We trust that the public spirit of the town will manifest itself, and that the citizens will subscribe enough capital to admit of the scheme being started on a sufficiently large scale tc insure its success. All can take part, and the surest guarantee of success will be found in the co- operation of all classes, each subscribing according to its ability. We fully recognise that the public will require time and opportunity to understand what is proposed. We have, therefore, decided to hold meetings at which those who have been actively engaged in similar undertakings will speak. The Beautiful Warrington Society have kindly consented to convene the first meeting. This will be held by the Mayor's courtesv,in the Council r of the Town Hall on Friday, eau\ December 7th, at ree o'clock in the afternoon, and those interested are vited to attend. The meeting will be addressed by _ r. Crossley Greenwood, Organ- izing Secretary of the Co-partnership Tenants' Housing Council.

GARDEN CITY.

In view of the proposal to establish Garden Suburbs in the neighbourhood of Warrington, the following lines,-which were written by Alderman Bennett after attending the Inaugural Ceremony at Garden Citt Letchworth, on the 9th October, 1903, and originally published in "The Daily News," will probably be read with interest :

Grd,m City.

I can hear the children singing in the city, Though the wind-swept fields are bare, And my eyes, which have so oft been moist with pity, In the dawning gladness share; I can see them, with their faces like blown roses, In the spacious streets at play, As the silence of a golden sunset closes, All the gladness of a golden day. I can see the stalwart sons of toil returning, With their bodies clean and strong, To the gardens where the ppppy-fires are burning, And the linnet sings his song; I can see sweet groups of comely women gather In the hush of twilight hours, And can hear the rose-lipped children murmuring As be toils amid the flowers. I." father," And, upon my path with joyful heart proceeding, I can see great sculptured porticos that stand In the shade of spacious highways softly leading To a "green and pleasant land " ; And I rest awhile within a crysta,l palace, Where perennial fountains play, And the water lily lifts its stainless chalice _To the gleaming rainbow spray. And the roar of busy loom, and wheel, and hammer, Comes like music from afar; And no_baleful glare of forges mocks the glamour Of the silver evening star; And no hideous maze of hell-dark chimneys vomit Their black insult to the sky; And the shining host of heaven peering from it, Seem to smile as I pass by. And in vain I look for vulgar taverns reeking In the hot streets of the town ; And I cannot find a slum for all my seeking. As I wander up and down ; But I bear a gust of music in the meadows, And the shouts of honest mirth, Or I watch the lovers strolling in the shadows Of a honeysackled earth. And the weariness, the sinfulness, the squalor, Of the slums and streets I know, And the poor young faces white with early pallor, And the footsteps sad and slow, And the witless curse, the laugh that rings so hollow, And the leer in joyless eyes, And the drunken sleep, the pauper's grave, that follow, And the children's miseties. Were a phantom of an age whose doom is written, Were a horror which I shudder to recall, Were a blot upon the sunny fame of Britain, Were a curse that smites us all; And with grateful eyes, and happy feet, I wander Through these autumn pathways sere, And I seem to see the Golden City yonder In the Garden City here.

a ea a a a a a I can see the people crowning from the alleys, And from reeking court and slum, To the freshness and the verdure of these valleys -- They are singing as they come. And the nightmare of the old life close; And the sickness and the heartache melt away, As they toil with willing hands amid the roses, Where the rainbow fountains play.

NEW CAFE FOR WARRINGTON.

A PROMISING ENTERPRISE.

Warrington people will be interested in an under- taking which has been launched this week under very promising auspices and bids fair to be a great success. It takes the form of a handsomely equipped cafe which has been opened at 39, Bridge-street, by a recently formed company known as Atkinson's Cafes Limited. The directors are : —Councillor Wilkinson (chairman), Mr. John Atkinson, ' Mrs. E. Atkinson, Messrs. T. Critchley, J. W. Kay, Joseph Wilkinson, J. Jackson, J. E. Richardson, and Albert Warburton, Mr. A.H. Clive being the secretary. The cafe occupies a commanding position and is an admirable addition to the architectural features of the town. The interior arrangements are of the. most attractive description and the decorations are in the best taste. On entering the visitor finds himself in the counter department which is furnished with wall fittings tastefully set out with fancy goods. A screen divides this room from the cafe proper which presents a very inviting appear- ance. There are five cubicles, upholstered in red leather, providing seating accommodation for thirty persons. The counter department and the cafe are choicely furnished in Dantzic oak inlaid with tulip wood and green and white ornamental woods. The screen partitioning the cafe from the kitchen is of Dantzic oak with inlay in accordance with the design of the building and is glazed with lead lights of very striking colours. The kitchen is most efficiently equipped, the cooking apparatus having been sup- plied by Messrs. Richmond, and there is every facility for indoor and outdoor catering. The general dining-room is on the first floor and will accommodate thirty-six persons. For special occasions, such as whist drives and private parties, the room is capable of seating fifty or sixty visitors. The .decorations are highly artistic and a more com- fortable dining room could not be desired. X hand- some well-lightod billiard-room, with up-to-date table, occupies the second floor. A novel feature is the smoke room in the basement. It has been arranged after the style of a room in an old castle and is a pleasant resort, the appearance being considerably improvedi by a greenhouse intro- duced at what would n ordinary bnsiness premises have served for the goods entrance. There are com- fortable settees and handsome oak tables with tops of beaten copper of special design. The heating and ventilation of the building are on the most approved lines and it is splendidly lighted by electricity. Mr. Albert Warburton, architect, Bold-street, is to be com- plimented on the admirable way in which he has designed and superintended the work. Messrs. W. Hodgkinson Ltd., have been responsible• for the furnishing which quite maintains their high reputation.

===== 0002601_19061201_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
1.13. t. OPENING DINNER.

At the invitation of the directors a large company of ladies and gentlemen assembled in the general dining- room on Tuesday evening for the opening dinner. The proceedings, which were highly enjoyable, were pre- sided over by Councillor Wilkinson. An excellent repast having been served, the usual loyal toasts were honoured.

Mr. ALBERT WARBURTON proposed the health of The Mayor and Mayoress and said that all who knew them accorded them the deepest respect and affection. (Applause.) The Mairott, in responding, said he had not sought the high position in which he had been placed but he was very proud to be chief magistrate of the old town and he hoped with God's help to do what was right and just towards all people. He apologised for the absence of the Mayoress on that occasion. Mr. E. RICHARDSON proposed "The town and trade of Warrington," and incidentally mentioned that the cafe stood on the site of the house in which he was born. On behalf of a large body of tradesmen he wished to express to the Mayor their gratification that he had succeeded to that high and responsible position. The present state of affairs called for judgment and tact and discrimination on the part of the Mayor, and they believed that in Alderman Smethurst they would find those qualities, and that the name of the old town would once more be rehabilitated in the neighbourhood. He had every reason to believe they were in for a pros- perous time in regard to trade in the town. (Applause). He was sure the remarks made by the Mayor when he was elected to the Mayoralty, regarding the need for better means of communication between the different parts of the town, were generally approved by the rate- payers. Something was urgently necessary to bring the different parts of the town more into touch with each another. (Hear, hear.) Another matter was that, whilst they cordially sympathized with the objects of the "Beautiful Warrington Society," the question of the, town's health required more serious consideration. He was afraid there was a tendency to overlook that side of the question: They wanted the town to be beautiful but they also wanted the people to live longer to enjoy it. He would like to see the stigma, that Warrington was the worst paved town in the kingdom, removed. (Hear, bet.r.) We were fortunate in having so many industries in the town and at no time had trade been altogether depressed. At present it was generally in a healthy condition, and he hoped the venture they were Inaugurating that evening would contribute in no small way towards the continued prosperity of the town. (Applause). - Councillor NIELD responded and said the Town Council had done their best to improve the appearance of the town. He thought that caf4 and similar ventures were a proof that the town was making remarkable progress. In trade he thought they were enjoying a degree of prosperity they had not known for years. He was an optimist quite as much as the German Emperor —(laughter)—for he believed that was the right note to strike. They wanted more optimism on the part of their manufacturers. He believed that to some extent they brought about trade depression themselves. When the Lord Mayor of Landon or someone else said we were going to have a slump in trade, people buttoned up their pockets with the result that a slump did come, retrenchment brought about the disaster prophesied. There could be nothing in the way of trade depression that could be really accounted for until all our wants were supplied,and in as much as our wants were multiply- ing fast and furious there was not likely to be any stagnation. He believed we were in for a degree of trade prosperity that we had not hitherto known and that the wakening up of Japan and China and other great countries would provide us with a trade, over and I above that which was require to meet our own demands, I which was calculated to fill us with optimism. He was afraid that up to a few few years ago manufacturers were not only behind in their equipmeat and scienti- fically but they seemed to be possessed with a spirit of despair. He believed they had acted on the Prince of / Wales' advice to "Wake up "and had realized that they I were not justified in being afraid of go-ahead countries ' like United States and Germany and had brought them- selves up-to-date in machinery and organization. , (Applause.) The MAYOR proposed "Prosperity to Atkinson's Cafes, Limited." He referred in highly eulogistic terms to Mr. and Mrs. Atkinson. He hoped the cafe would be a great success and that it would be no hindrance to any similar establishment in the town. They had a good chairman, a good secretary and a good board of directors. (Hear, hear.) Mr. ATKINSON replied and said that Mrs. Atkinson and himself were indebted for the expressions of con- fidence in their ability to manage the undertaking. He ' did not think it would fall short of success through want of effort on their part. (Applause.) Mr. JACKSON submitted "The Contractors," and I remarked that their work had in Ivory respect afforded great satisfcction. (Hear, hear.) Mr. W. RODGKINSON, in responding, said that for the success of his part of the work he owed thanks to his employees for the conscientious performance of their work. He congratulated the Cafe Company on their ' enterprise ; it reflected considerable credit upon them, and the town should be thankful to them for what they had done. In that venture the company had done more ' for the town than had the " spouting" in the town during the last two or three years. (Hear, bear.) Warrington bad been lacking for many years in attrac- tive features and it rested with the inhabitants to make it more inviting. Such establishments as the new cafe assisted materially to make the town attractive to possible visitors, and therefore induced more trade. (Hear, hear). Mr. F. WARBURTON also responded. Mr. E. DRAPER proposed the health of " The Chair- man" and said that in Councillor Wilkinson the company had an asset of considerable value. If he devoted the same amount of attention to the affairs of the company as he did to Corporation work, there need be little doubt as to its success. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN briefly responded. During the evening songs were creditably rendered by Miss Armstrong, NI i 49 May Atkinson, Mr. F. Timperley and Mr. B. Poole. Mr. G. Lomas acted as accompanist.

WARRINGTON.

The Ede'sten Action. On Moniay the case Of Colonel Edelsten v. ths Warrington Corporation was mentioned to Vice- Chancellor Leigh-Clare at Liverpopl. Mr. Laurence, representing Colonel Edelsten, stated in the presence of Mr. Stewart Deakin (for the Corporation) and Mr. Rutherford (for Alderman Balton), that the parties had agreed to a settlement, and his Lordship made an order according to the terms of settlement.

LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET:AdVt. Spiritualism. . . . .

'Special services were held at the Spiritualist Society's room, Druid's Hall, on Sunday, when addresses were given by Miss Ruth Sage, of Nelson. In the afternoon there was a good audience, but in the evening the hall was crowded, when Miss Sage gave an ddress on "When and how shill we worship God?" followed by a few clairvoyant descriptions. During the service Mr. E. R. Roberts, of Runcorn, effectively rendered "The better lanl " and " Holy City." Mr. Joseph Foster accompanied on the organ. On Monday evening Miss Sage, after a brief address, gave a number of clairvoyant descriptions, being suc- cessful in the majority of cases in obtaining recognition. Popular Penny Concerts. There was again a crowded attendance at the weekly popular penny concert at Bold-street Wesleyan Schoolroom, on Saturday evening, when an enjoyable programme was gone through. Mr. Holland presided. Songs were given with effect by Mrs. Hansen, Miss Carrie Hastings, Mr. J. E. Hannan and Mr. J.Osborne, and a duet by Mr. and Mrs. Hannan was warmly applauded. Several conjuring tricks by Mr. Brown pleased the audience. The humorist was Mr. E. A. Sanders, who provoked much laughter with his recitations. Durum an interval in the programme the Chairman and the Rev. J. Manning referred to the great loss the town had sustained by the death of Councillor Brough, and expressed sorrow for the bereaved family. New Sale Rooms. Warringtonians will remember the old "Swan Hotel "which for many years flourished in the rear of Bridge-street. Some years ago the license of the premises was allowed to lapse and since then the building has undergone considerable alteration and improvement. This has been done at the instigation of Mr. Robert Lowe, B3nk-street, who has adapted the Hotel to the requirements of his business and opened it as a saleroom and warehouse. On the ground floor a large Saleroom has been constructed in which Mr. Lowe s weekly sales will be conducted whilst, the remaining fifteen rooms afford ample accommodation for the storage of goods. The Swan Rooms are easily accessible from Bridge-street, and being central should prove very popular. Mr. Lowe's premises are unique in that they comprise a bit of old Warrington in an excellent state of preservation and afford an interest- ing link between the present and the past. The total area is about 1,000 square yards.

Warrington's Appreciation of Browning. The last of the series of University Extension lectures on" Robert Browning " was delivered in the Co-operative Hall on Wednesday evening by Mr. J. A. Dale, M.A. The lecturer again dealt with "The Ring and the book " and read numerous extracts. He pointed out that the blank verse cvhich Browning used in the poem was extraordinarily uneven, partly by intention and partly by carelessness, but at its best it would be hard to match it anywhere else.—At the close of the lecture Canon Morley Stevenson moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Dale. He was afraid they must sorrowfully confess that to the multitude of people Browning was still not a popular poet and to that must be ascribed the fact that the lectures had not been more largely attended. The lectures had done a great deal to show them that it was worth while to study Browning and to love him. Mr. Dale had mintcd out bow much lay beneath the somewhat rugged style and the obscurity of certain passages, and had shown that it was worth while to dig as they would be sure to get the true metal out of the mine.—Alderman Bertnett seconded. He said he counted about a dozen men in the audience, so that out of 70,000 inhabitants, obviously only about a dozen men thought it worth while to spend an evening listening to a lecture on such a great poet as Browning. It was immensely to the credit of the ladies that they were better represented. Want of interest in poetry was greatly to be deprecated but Browning required more understand- ing than most of our poets. Be, however, only needed to be known to be loved. In this somewhat pessimistic and indifferent age, it was inspiring to find's poet who was so splendidly optimistic.—The vote was heartily I accorded and in responding the lecturer said there were other poets who ranked higher than Browning, bat the influence that Browning had exercised on him had been enormous. With all his faults, when they got'to grips w!th Browning, they were at grips with a man, and a man of tremendous strength and inspiring force.

UNDER via CLocur for Good BOOTS. Advt.— St. Clement's Mission Billiard Club. A most successful smoking concert in connection with this club, under the chairmanship of the Rev. T. W. Barber, was held on Tuesday evening, when a large number of members and friends were enter- tained. The most important item of the evening was the distribution of prizes for the recent Billiard Handicap, to the successful competitors. Before per- forming this function the Rev. T. W. Barber referred to the benefits such institutions conferred upon the community of young men. He said that firstly they acted as a counteracting influence to public-houses, inasmuch as the temptation of friends who might possibly frequent the billiard rooms of such places was to a great extent dispelled. Clubs carried on on teetotal lines had no possibility of any harm. His second reason was, that they caused good feeling and sociability. He thought that that was especially the case in a district like that surrounding St. Clement's Mission. The club formed a centre, where men got to know each other better, and it would be a very great loss if the men went elsewhere for their games. His third poist, was that a Club of that description should be an attachment between men and , the Church. He, personally, very much valued the I intercourse which he obtained with the members of that club and he should like all of them to make a point of returning the compliment, and come to the Mission as often as possible, when they would be sure of a hearty welcome. The prizes were then distri- buted as bllows : —l, A. Proverbs; 2, P, H. Siinkin ; 3, H. Rough; 4, J. Fleming; 5, H. Broadhurst ; 6, T. Gandy. During the evening songs were well rendered by Messrs. J. Wolfenden, F. Sharp, C. .1 Sheath, J. Gray. H. Simkin, F. Miller, John Wainwright, H C. Rhoden and T. Davies, the accompanists being Messrs. T. Davies and J. Wolfenden. After a vote of thanks to the Chairman a most pleasant evening was concluded by the singing of the National Anthem.

" HERALD " KITCHEN RANGES and " Bayern " Fire Grates are hard to beat.—Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Junior Liberal Association.

The weekly meeting of the above association was held on Monday evening in the Liberal Association Rooms, Bewsey-street, when reviews were given from magazines by members of the Review. Committee. Mr. J. Hawthorn presided. Mr. H. Rathbone reviewed an article on "Is the party system honest ?" The writer argued in the negative, and made a plea for what he called groups. The party system created discreditable scenes, and the hi-party system had been in times past, and might be again in the future, the victim of the of the ve., same weaknesses which they so much deplored in foreign Parliaments.. It was impossible for one man to honestly subscribe to the whole Conservative or the whole Liberal fashion of faith, and tl-e group system would greatly facilitate the work of committees. In conclusion, he said that the party system was demoralizing and outworn.—A. review on "Old-age pensions " was then read by Mr. H. Gray (secretary), in which he stated that since the introduction of old-age pensions wages had been increased, shorter hours had been made, and the general condition of the workpeople was better. In 1890, 41,000 persons between the age of 60 and 65, and almost a quarter of a mil!ion over the age of 65, were receiving parish pay. It had been stated that poverty in old age was due to vice, intemperance and thriftlessness, but from facts elicited he found that that was not the case. It had been proved that hundreds of thousands of people who had served many years for their country were left destitute iu their old- age through no fault of•their own, and they could only be dealt with by a universal pension ,scheme. (Applause.) Mr. Gray then gave a review "Blind leaders of the blind," from the "National Review," which dealt in an able manner with the Government's attitude towards reduction in naval and military estimates. A short discussion afterwards took place,

GASTRIC INFLUENZA.

Bile Beans Banish Severe Pains.

Gastric influenza is very prevalent to-day. It is due to low inflamation of the stomach, and is often one of the after-effects of an attack of inflame, or a severe cold. Bile Beans are an ideal specific for troubles of this nature.

The most prominent symptoms are shooting pains between the shoulders, heaviness and heat at ,the pit of the stomach after food, nausea, irregular appetite, frontal headaches, great weariness, low spirits, and wasting. Bile Beans have achieved wonderful results in many cases of gastric influenza, the pare herbal extracts of which Bile Beans are wholiy composed having the effect of restoring the normal healthy condition of the inflamed linings of the stomach and bowels, regulating the secretion of the gastric juices, and setting the delicate machinery of digestion and nutrition in perfect working order. Miss Emily Walker, of 6, Tenham Avenue, Streatham Hill, S.W., says February I was attacked by gastric influenza. I had severe pains in the side after the fever left me, and I thought it was an attack of I ulceration of the stomach, which I had had some years previously. I could not lie down in bed, nor could I ' cough or sneeze without enduring great pain. As for sleep or rest, that was impossible. "At last I made up my mind to give Bile Beans a trial, and am glad I did so, for I found that they gave me great relief. I kept on with Bile Beans until I was I quite better. Since I have taken Bile Beans my general health has been much better." Bile Beans for Bilioulness may be obtained of all chemists, at is. lid. per box, or 2s. 9d. for box contain- ing 3 times the quantity. Refuse cheap substitutes, and insist on the exact medicine that has proved to yonr friends it can always be relied upon as a cure.

OUR. LOCAL CHURCHES.

United Bible Class_ Services.

Some 450 men attended a united service in connection with the Church of England Bible classes at Warring- ton which was held on ISunday afternoon in St. Paul's Church. The preacher was the Rev. E. IL Tarbuck (vicar of St. George's, Altrincham, and a former Vicar of St. Paul's Church), who took for his text the words: "That I may know Him and the power of His ressurrec- tion," Philippians iii. part of tenth verse. The rev. gentleman commented upon the importance of unity, and said it was a splendid idea on the part of the mem- bers of the Church of England Bible classes in Warring- ton to join together and to be thus united in Church work. Churchmen had already set the lead with regard to the education question, and he looked to the present service as being part of a great forward movement. The collection was in aid of the church renovation fund.

incliffe P.S.A. Mr: B. Farrar, the hon. secretary of the Wyoliffe P.S.A. Society, presided over the meeting held on Sunday and spoke upon "Personal effort." It was, he said, well for each one to ask himself if he had done everything he could for the prosperity of the Brother- hood. Although they could not fully estimate the results of their efforts, as they were called upon to do and be, a large gathering gave a bigger sphere of use- fulness. At the same time the biggest good was not obtained from the largest assemblies. They must not despise small beginnings, as it was God who gave the result. He referred to Mr. Spurgeon's small beginning* , in a Methodist Chapel. and the great good accomplished since in the name of his Lord and Master. Mr. Barbery, of Oldham, gave an interesting address upon " Bringing in the sheaves," and drew some useful lessons from the ancient temple in the days of our Lord, reminding the audience that then, and since, the sheaves had and would bebrought in to the honour of the Lord. Mr. C. Cotton also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Payton pre- ided at the organ.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. On Sunday morning the Rev. Joseph Manning prelched from the text "Put not a stumbling block in the way of my people." The last Sunday in November is always recognised by the \ Methodist Churches as Temperance Sunday, so Mr. Manning made special reference to the evils of drinking and gambling in our midst. All adult abstainers *were asked to leave their names in the porch on quitting the building, so that an estimate of their numbers might be formed. In the evening the Rev. John Gunnell preached from the text "And the Angel of the Lord appeared unto the woman and said unto her 'Now therefore beware, I pray thee and drink not wine nor strong drink,'" Judges xiii. v. 3 and 4. His theme was the terrible increase of drinking habits among women—secret drinking in many cases, rendered possible by grocers' licences. He reminded his hearers that the English. as a race, had been saved from physical degeneration, though all these ages of heavy drinking among the men brthe sobriety of the women, and mentioned John Burn's recent words of advice to the working classes, that they should attempt to amelior- ate their own conditions by striving to overcome their growing habits of excessive drinking and gambling. At the Wesleyan Guild on Monday night Mr. W. Silcock gave a lecture 3n "The rise and progress of English literature." Mr. E. J. Guest, B.A.,J.f.;presided. Mr. J. Upson proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and Lecturer which was a heartily accorded.

The Founder of the Y.M.C.A. Sermon by the Rev. John Yonge.

Preaching at Wycliffe Church, on Sunday evening, the Rev. John Yonge took for his subject, "A true Christian Knight: Lessons from the Life of Sir George Williams, founder of the Y.111.C.A." The text was selected from Philippians i. part of the 21st verse: "To me to live is Christ." The rev, gentleman, at the outset, pointed out that he had been reading with the deepest interest the recently published memoir of Sir George Williams, who, amid every token of public honour, was buried in St. Paul's Cathedral in November of last year. He happened to be in London on the day of the funeral, and was passing across the foot of Ludgate-hill, when he found the busy traffic stopped by a long procession of carriages conveying the great company gathered to pay the deceased the last earthly honours. The spectacle might have led an onlooker to suppose that some personage belonging to the highest rank of nobility, of art, letters, science or war was being carried to his grave. It was related that a French woman in the crowd as she looked on the scene asked "Who is this? Was hes, very rich man?" "Yes," said one who stood by her, "he was a man very rich in good works." In actual fact it was the funeral of a most successful tradesman and merchant, of one who had risen from the ranks, beginning his career, however, and pursuing it to the end with the supreme resolve that he would be first, foremost, and always a Christian. He devoted his time, energy and opportunity and, as he gained them' his influence and his wealth, to the supreme service of Jesus Christ. It was that fact of his life which had compelled these high honours of sepulture in the Metropolitan Cathedral, amid so vast a concodrso of men of all parties and sects and classes. Sir George Williams created for him- self a name, held in high honour all over the world as the founder of the Y.NI.C.A., and it would be a matter of regret to some at any rate that that cosmo- politan institution should never have gained favourable or permanent establishment in Warrington. But be not wish to dwell at any length on the Y.M.C.A. It I might be said that its history was not without instances 1 of failure. It might be deemed by some that certain of its rules and regulations and theological ideas were I cramped and narrow but they might freely speak of it as a splendid development of a splendid idea, affording flee testimony to the good that might come from a young men's prayer meeting. The preacher gave an interesting sketch of the life of the founder of the Y.H.C.A., from which many valuable lessons were derived. His biographer said of Sir George that be did not attempt to serve God and mammon— "He served God and made mammon serve him," "I conceive it to be my duty," he said, "as God gives me grace to do it, to sanctify everything in the Lord, to bay and sell, to engage young men, to pay them their salaries, to give them social comforts, all as to the Lord," and his whole life attested his sincerity in those I high ideals. Sir George was one of those very few men who, although living a busy, many-sided life, had a , heart which always found leisure from itself, to soothe ' and sympathise. His greatest joy was to do something to brighten ether lives. The preacher, in conclusion, called the attention of young men to that glorious servant of Jestis Christ in tbe hope that it might awaken I the desire to imitate his life of devoted consecration. The supreme thing his example taught was the infinite value of Christian whole-heartedness. There was no doubt in his (rr. Yonge's) mind of the entire devotion of George Williams to his Saviour. He fully illustrated one of his own frequent sayings,— "that the great thing in religion was the conlplete capture and conse- cration of the heart," and he believed his life taught how decision for Christ and devotion to the service of others settled all the desires and satisfied all the wishes even of ardent young men. There was no pursuit in life so beneficent to a man as the life of a true disciple- ship, nothing that could matte life so blessed as living not unto themselves but into others.

To EVERY LOVING NIECE AND

, g This week my post-bag, I .am pleased to say, has been exceptionally heavy, and its con- tents varied. I am very, very grateful for the Way in which many of you have been compet- ing in making useful little articles for our needy friends this coming winter. The prizes this week go to the following Nieces :—Margaret Mary Press and Jennie Cummins, the first- named having sent in a nice warm pair of stays for some fortunate little babe, also a Short Story, entitled " Gertie and the Gipsies," which you will find further on in the Column. Jennie receives her prize for the contribution of three very useful scarves. As I said before, I am glad to see tlie interest taken in this work, and it shows that you have a warm corner in your hearts for those less fortunate than yourselves. So any of you dear children who have not yet helped us will perhaps give it a thought at any early date, and, to use a very old phrase, "the smallest contribution will be thankfully received." Who has solved the problem as to what the people in Scotland do when it rains? . . . GERTIE AND THE GIPSIES. CHAPTER I.

It was the eve of Gertie's birthday, and Mrs. Furlong said to her little girl, "Come, Gertie, dear, and, let me put your hat on, and we will go and look at the shops and see if there is anything pretty in them." The two then went out, ind directed their steps towards the High Street. They had not gone far when they came to a large toy shop. In the window was a ;lovely large doll, dressed in silk, and sitting in a perambulator. Mrs. Furlong thought this would do beautifully'for a birthday present for Gertie, and telling her to wait a few minutes outside the slop, hurried in to order the doll and perambulator. to be sent to her address as soon as possible dWhen she came out of the shop Gertie was nowhere to be seen. "That naughty child, I told her not to go away from this window," Mrs. Furlong said to herself; "I wonder where she can be," and looked up and down the street, but could see no trace of Gertie. Then she began to get frightened, and asked everybody if they had seen anything of a little girl in a white silk dress, with brown curls. But everybody said "No." Then Mrs. Furlong hurried to the Police Station and told the police what had happened, and they said she must have been stolen. Policemen were sent in all directions to find her, but it was no good. and at last they had to give up the attempt. Poor Mrs. Furlong was beside herself with grief. CHAPTER 11.

While Mrs. Furi-o-n—g-la—d--be-e-n.-in the toy shop, a gipsies' van had come along, and all of a sudden one of the men jumped down, stuffed something in Gertie's mouth so that she couldn't scream, and the next minute had her safely in the Van. The horses were put to all possible speed, and Gertie was soon far away from her mother. When they had got out of the town and were a long way off on a country road, the horses slackened speed a bit, and the gag was taken out of Gertie's mouth. Gertie immediately started screaming, but one of the gipsies said. to her, "Now, my girl, just stop that noisg, for it's no good, nobody can hear you," and so Gertie thought she had better stop. Then her nice gold chain and cross, which she wore round her neck, was taken off, and all her nice clothes and shoes and stock- ii,gs were taken off, and dirty, untidy things, were put on instead, so that poor Gertie looked quite a different child. After .this the gipsies went from one •town to another, and the way they earned their living wai by selling brushes. For the first month or so Gertie was kept well iwide the van, but after that the gipsies thought perhaps she would be safe to go round with them selling brushes or baskets. And so time flies, nobody ever recOgnising poor Gertie, until at last A year had fled since she had been:

—t last the pvies thought, as Gc .1 been very good, %hey could trust her ..ione, and so it g6t to be quit. ). commi thing to send her out selling baskets by hi self, or to send her out to get anything th wanted themselves. One day Gertie was goil round as usual sellmg baskets when she car to a certain house where two ladies were sittin One was in black the other was dressed green. When Gertie saw them she said to hi

out Lou Ler- ing

sell, "I am sure that lady in black is my mother. I will knock at the door and ask them if they will buy a basket, and see if she recog- nises mg." And-me knocked at the door. The two ladies then looked up, and directly the lady in black saw who it was she fainted. The lady in green was astounded, and picked her friend up, then rang the bell and ordered some brandy and gave it to Mrs. Furlong (for it was indeed Gertie's mother), who on revived. Mrs. Furlong then begged Mrs. Longdon to let her child in and bring her to her at once. Gertie was then brought in, and after they had kissed , ever so many times, she had to tell how she had been stolen, and what she had been doing ever since. A message was then sent at once to the police, telling all %bout it, ana-saying where the gipsies were. Mrs. Furlong had moved, and the other lady was a friend stay- • ing with her, and she had gone into mourning for her child, thinking she would never see her again. Gertie was now taken upstairs, and - her dirty clothes were taken off her and burned, and after having a nice hot bath, some nice clean clothes were put on her. After that they had tea, and then Gertie was taken into a room. where all the presents she had had given her for her birthday when she had been stolen were laid out on the bed. Gertie was delighted with them all, but especially with the doll and perambulator.

When the police got the message, they hurried off after the gipsies, who were put in prison for a long time as a penalty.

Dear Ulmle Ned,—l should very much like to become a member of your "Children's Corner." Please accept the following tale.—l remain, your loving niece.

. - BEATRICE IVIIITAXER. TAKING DOLLIE TO THE PAWNBROKER'S.

There are a great many pathetic incidents connected with the lives of poor children that are never heard of outside of the immediate vicinity. A few days since a little girl, who had - - seen her mother take a ring and other valuables to the pawnbrokers to get money to buy medi- cine and food while her father was sick and out of work, tried to pawn her dolly. The mother was taken sick from overwork and care of the father, and told the little girl that she did not know how they would get anything to eat. The year before this the little girl had re- ceived the doll from the Christmas tree at the mission school. She had taken great pleasure in it, but it was all she had, and the only thing she could take to the pawnbroker's to get some money on. She wrapped up the dolly in a piece of Taper and went to the shop. The pawnbroker was very angry when he unrolled the package and saw the dolly. The paint was off its face, and the hair was in a tangle; the dress, also, was very much soiled. The pawnbroker was a heartless man. "Get out," he said, throwing the doll on the floor, and breaking it to pieces. A lady who was passing the door chanced to see the man throw the doll down, and when the little girl came out of the shop crying, she said, "What is the matter, little girl I" The child told her pathetic story, and the lady, who was a district visitor, went home with her. When she found that the little girl's story was true, she told it to some of her friends, /4/0 and they helped the hamily inn's° many good ways that the father and mother soon got well, and the father got a place where he could earn c' enough to get necessary food and clothes for those he loved so well. And you will all be glad to know that they gave the little girl a, new doll, too. This is a true story, told to me by a friend. —tk, Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you aispiece of poetry, hoping you will like it!.• it my liketlt thistrY ands e lll ht Yours truly, thought ga styooru would lncloksee a i jise.c e ELSIE TUBBY.
