WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

ANNUAL TEA IYIEETING.

The annual tea meeting in connection with the War- rington Women's Liberal Association was held on Monday evening in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street. This was a new departure brought about by the very largely increased membership of the Association. Previously the annual gatherings had been held at the Liberal Club but more accornodation was essential this year. Nearly three hundred members assembled, and enjoyed an excellent tea which was catered for by Atkinson's Cafes,Limited. Amongst those presvat were —.Mesdames Armitage, Parker, Garnett, O'Brien, Clegg, Jackson Holmes, Guest, Clay, Walker, Lindley, Owen. Hitchen, Osborne, J. Maginn, F. Maginn, Hewitt, Dutton ; Miss Crosileld, Miss Dutton, Miss Hawthorne, Miss Guest, Miss Gornall, Miss Walker, and Miss Short. _ _

After tea a meeting was held to hear an address on " Free Trade" by Miss IC Ashworth Briggs (Women's Free Trade Union, London). Miss Crosfield presided and she was supported on the platform 1337 Mrs. Armitage, Mrs. Walker, Mrs. Holmes, Mrs. Parker, Mrs. Garnett, Mrs. Osborne and Miss Dutton. _ CURRENT POLITICS. _

I Miss CROSFIELD referred to the present Govern- ment's programme. She said there was that useful measure which songht to provide decent eottages, and property allotments for the poverty stricken peasants who were so ill-housed in the west of Ireland. That was the sort of Bill which authorities on the question said ought to be applied to England and Wales. Next year they might hope to see a measure brought into Parliament to enable small holdings to be held by the agricultural labourer, whilst at the present time Lord Carrington, who was one of the finest ministers of agri- chlture they had ever had, was utilizing Crown lands for small holdings. Another Act was oae concerning which all political parties agreed, namely the Corrupt Practices Act, which prohibited secret commissions in business, whilst another measure would allow cheaper postage rates for the bulky literature used by the blind. It was hoped that before Christmas they would get through the three great Labour Bills, namely, the Trades Disputes Bill, an extension of the Workmen's Compensation Act, and the Shipping Bill. (Applause). Parliament desired to extend the Workmen's Compensation Act to agriculturists and seamen, and Mr. Lloyd-George was engaged with the Shipping Bill, which was an exceptionally good one. (Applause.) The latter would ensure the greater safety and better accommodation of British seamen. They had far too many foreigners engaged on British ships, and it was calculated that if better accommodation were provided Englishmen would be encouraged to man English ships. The Bill also provided that all foreign ships which entered English ports must conform to the English standard of safety in regard to the amount of their cargo. It was not fair that foreign ships should come to our ports overloaded. (Applause.) Then there was the Education Bill. She thought that most of them hoped that the House of Lords would not render it impossible to come to a friendly settlement on that question. (Applause.) Any- one in his or her right senses could not wish to see secular education brought into England. Religious faith was the foundation of life, and no one could wish for his children to be brought up without religious teaching. (Applause.) The Land Tenure Bill was also before Parliament—a Bill that one might call "the famers' charter." The Budget showed how well the Chancellor of the Exchequer's predecessor managed affairs when it was found that there was a surplus of three millions, which was being devoted to the abolition of the coal tax, the reduction of the tax on tea, the reduction of the national debt and the assistance of the unemployed. (Applause.) She thought they might honestly say that was trying to curb expense in every possiale way. (Hear, hear.) It was also refreshing to find that the Priam Minister had said that as soon as time and money would permit old-age pensions would be secured to the people of the country. (Applause.) They owed a great deal to Sir H. Compbell-Bannerman. (Hear, hear.) It was said that he had a warm heart and a cool head, and he must be beloved by all who believed in progress. (Applause.) Miss BIRCHALL sang "Winter " with taste, Miss Gertude Henshall gave a recitation and 'Airs. Jackson Holmes contributed a pianoforte solo with great ability. _

===== 0002601_19061201_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN AND FREE TRADE.

Miss Astr'NVORT-11- Bivas than addressed the com- pany on" Free Trade" a subject which she contrived to make both intelligible and interesting to the women present. She first of all spoke of the objects of the Women's Free Trade Union. She said it was formed when Mr. Chamberlain first proposed a tax on bread, because they regarded that as a woman's question.. (Applause.) Housekeeping employed the most hands in the world, and when they touched that they upset all the homes in the land. It was in the cities of Lancashire that Free Trade was started; Lancashire found the money for Cobden's agita- tion against the bread tax. It was said in those times that the want of work killed the men, but two years after Free Trade had been estab- lished it was found that the life of the workingmen was lengthened by two years,and that of the women by three years.lt was Protection that shortened life.(Applause.) A tax on imports meant increased cost in housekeeping, increased the rent and rates, and increased the number of unemployed. Imports were things they had to buy. They were given in exchange for the goods exported. The speaker instanced the conditions of life in Germany to illustrate her contentious. The Liberal Government was going to tackle the land question with which was bound up the question of rent. Rates and rents were very high because in the first place there were too many people in the towns. If more lived in the country there would be less competition for houses. It was not so easy, however, to take people back to the land as many suggested. People should be encouraged to stay on the land and their own children would fill up the gaps. (Applause.) Having alluded to the Lords' opposition to the Land Tenure Bill, the speaker mentioned the rejection, by the Lords, of the Labour Members' Aliens Act, and the taxation of land values. All the wealth of England was going into the landlords' pockets, England belonged to the English, and why should they be obliged to pay such increasingly heavy rents for permission to live in Eng- land ? (Hear, hoar.) The man who sat still and raked, in the money should bear his share of the burden. Seventy per cent. of the country's population lived in the towns. More people were required for the land because the land was not producing anything like what it should do in the way of food. In France, for instance, the peasants, who had allotments, obtained ten times as much produce from the cultivation of their land-as was obtained in this country. The Prime Minister hut set himself to create those small farmers in England. It was because there were so many people in the towns that there was so much unemployment. Alias Briggs contended that the Loidlords did not bear thei r full share of the rates and taxes. (Applause) On the proposition of Mrs. PARKER, seconded by Miss DUTTON, a vote of thanks was accorded Miss Ash- worth Briggs, and a similar compliment was paid Miss Crosfield, on the motion of Mrs. ARNIITA GE. Other ladies who had assisted were also thanked.

Hunslet v. Warrington.

Warrington appeared at Hunslet on Saturday,and were beaten by 6 points. The ganae, favonred as it was with beautifully fine weather, attracted a crowd of about 4,000 spectators. Warrington were without Fish, who was suffering from a somewhat severe attack of bronchitis, and Lees, their places being filled by Cart- wright and Jordan. Hunslet—Place, back; Hople, Eagers, W. Goldthorpe and Farrar, threequarter backs ; Jackson and A. E Goldthorpe, half backs; Higson, Jukes, Wilson. Walsh Cappleman and Shooter, forwards. Warrington—Berry, back ; Cartwright, Taylor, Dickinson and Brooks, threequarter backs ; Jordan and Hockenhall, half backs ; Preston, Boardman, Shug.ars, Naylor, Thomas and Harmer, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. McCutcheon,Oldham. Touch judges Messrs. F. T. Rowlands, Bradford, and H. Farrar, ' Keighley. Hunslet had the opening attack, and within a quarter of an hour had eight points on the board. A scrummago had been formed at the Warrington " " from which the ball came out to Albert Goldthorpe. He gave it to Jackson, who in turn transferred to Farrar. The last named beat Brooks, snd scored a clever try. The place was too far out fo'r Albert Goldthorpe to land a goal. Hunslet continued to press, and as the result of a strong rush Shooter scored a lucky try. Walter Goldthorpe failed at goal. It should be mentioned that this rush was the result of a brilliant burst by Eagers. A few minutes later Albert Goldthorpe put Hunslet still further ahead by drooping a neat goal from behind a scrummage. Warrington now had a look in, and Hunslet had their lead reduced. From a scrum- mage at the half-way line the ball came out to Hocken- hall, who kicked across, and Dickinson sprinting up, took the ball cleverely on the run, beat Eagers, and scored a fine try. Preston's shot at goal was a failure. Each goal was attacked in turn after this, but nothing was scored before half-time. Thus Hunslet crossed over leading by five points. The game reopened in fluctuating fashion. Hunslet had a promising attack early on, but they were driven back by a tall kick on the part of Taylor, which gained half the length of the field. The Hunslet forwards rushed the game back to the Warrington line, and from the succeeding scrummage Albert Goldthorpe forced, his way over, also adding an easy goal. This was immediately followed by a cleverly dropped goal on the part of Taylor. Thus encouraged, Warrington worked hard, and after a strong attack Brooks dashad round the " blind " side of a scrummage and scored a neat try, Preston adding a goal. There was still time for Jackson to dash over with a try for Hunslet. This was not improved upon. The whistle sounded a minute later, and Hunslet thus won the match by 2 goals and 4 tries (16 points) to Warrington 2 goals and 2 tries (10 points.)

===== 0002601_19061201_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN v. BARROW.

At Runcorn, before 1,500 spectators. In the opening stages the visitors attacked, but a round of passing between Jolley,Butterworth, and Deakin quickly trans- ferred play to the visitors' end, where from a rush by the home forwards Padbury got possession and scored, Richardson adding a goal. Langley followed with another try a moment later,Richardson failing with the goal kick. Wharton and Fallowfleld gained ground for Barrow, but a fine display of passing by Jolley, Watton Padbury, and Moran resulted in the latter scoring, Richardson again failing to improve. A free kick to Barrow followed, from which Bowker easily landed a goal. Before the interval Moran and Padbury scored tries for Runcorn, Jolley adding a goal from the first ' one. Half-time score :—Runcorn 2 goals, 5 tries (19 points), Barrow 1 goal (2 points). After the interval Barrow attacked for several minutes but were gradually forced back. From a free kick however, Bowker placed another goal for the visitors, but immediately on resumption Watton passed to Walker, who scored under the posts, Jolley failing at goal. Play was then contested near the Runcorn line, where Deakin got possession, and running almost the 'length of the field scored a fine try, Jolley adding a goal. • Buttorworth and Flanagan quickly added tries, which were improved by Jolley. The visitors then got into the home half, and Hannah smartly getting hold was over the Runcorn line before the home backs could I recover themselves, Bowker failing at goal. Near the close Richardson and Padbury each scored a try, Jolley failing with the goal kicks. Result :—Runcorn 5 goals 11 tries (43 points), Barrow 2 goals, 1 try (7 points). Teams :

Runcorn—Harrison, back ; Deakin, Butterworth Moran and Flanagan, three-quarter backs ; Jolley and Watton, half backs ; Walker, Padbury, Richardson, Kennedy, Dixon, and Langley, forwards. Barrow—Gifford, back : Wilson, Bowker, Brockbank and Bamber, three-quarter hacks ; Wharton and Fallow- field, half-backs ; Brough, Brown, Bowers, Hannah, Morgan and Jardine, forwards. Referee, Mr. F. Kennedy, Broughton Rangers. Touch judges, Messrs. A. Jones, Warrington, and W. Nevins, Warrington.

Warrington v. Swinton. The postponed match between Warrington and Swin- ton waS played at Warrington on Monday afternoon before 2,000 spectators. Neither side was at full strength, but the homesters won as they liked in the closing stages of a rather fast game. Teams and officials : . . _

----- Warrington : Knight, full back ; Landor, Dickenson, Taylor and Brooks, three-quorters ' • Hockenhall and Jordan, half backs ; Boardman, Preston, Thomas, Shugars, Heath and Naylor, forwards. Swinton : Dixon, full back ; Blakeley, Wallbrook, Simister and Jones, three quarters ; Booth and Bradley, half backs; Davies, Coletrup, Pomfret, Cheetham, Hob- son and Lloyd, forwards. Referee, Mr. W. 11I'Cutcheon (Oldham). Warrington started off in strong style and were quickly on the attack. Dixon repelling the invasion by some fine full back work. Tall kicking by the visitors ' eased the pressure slightly, but improvement was shown when Simister dribbled down the open, and the ball was forced passed Knight. Warrington experienced a warm time of it, end the visitors had the line at their mercy when Wallwork got offside and spoiled a favour- able opening. A free kick drove Swinton out, and from a penalty Preston essayed a shot at goal, the ball, how- ever, travelling low and beneath the crossbar. Warring- ton played up and Taylor made a bold but unsuccessful attempt to break through. Hockenhall next got clear but absence of finish spoiled the effort. Landor, the Warrington left wing, did some smart work on the left, and almost overcame Jones in a sharp sprint. The last-named, horcever, made a good recovery, and sprint- ing along touch, lorced Warrington to fall back. Thomas, however, got well away, and Taylor joining issue, the Swinton backs had all their work cut out to maintain their lino intact, Brooks just failed to take a pass from Dickenson when practically all opposition had been overcome. The want of finish on the part of the Warrington backs was very disappointing, and when Hockenhall got away down the open, and his pass went astray, the spectators cheered ironically. A couple of failures to place goals by Preston from penalties did not assist matters much, whilst an attempt by Knight to gain a substantial point did not meet with any better fate. Swinton were decidedly fortunate in repelling the many and varied attempts to cross, and it was not until near the interval that a sharp movement between Hockenhall and Jordan led up to Landor signalizing his debut in first class football, by dashing over for the first try at the left corner. The goal kick was a failure. Swinton,however, would not be denied, and the outcome of some smart movements caused Knight to kick dead from the field of play. From the scrummage Hockenhall cleared and had the better of an exchange with Dixon. At half time the score was three points in Warrington's favour Resuming the second half the locals were soon let Idown by Knight, who failed to take a long kick from Dixon, and the " Wirepullers" were in difficulties. A long dribble by Naylor speedily placed Warrington in neutral quarters.and Dixon nipped in finely and checked progress, forcing Warrington back, and causing Knight to adopt defensive tactics. The locals rallied and again attacking Thomas got possession near the line and crossed. Preston missing the goal. The Warringtonians were now exhibiting their proper form, and not many 1 minutes had elapsed before a sharp movement put Brooks in possession, and the Swinton gcal had another

narrow escape. Taylor forced the ball over the line, and Brooks, racing up at top speed, grounded the ball ' whilst Dixon was id the act of kicking dead. Knight kicked a goal. Dickenson followed with a try after a strong run, and Knight added another goal, whilst shaxp passing led up to Brooks notching his second try. Swinton continued to be overplayed, and in the last few minutes Boardman and Jordan completed excellent tries, and in each instance Preston added goals. Final score: Warrington, four goals and seven tries (29 points) Swinton nil.

Friendly Match:

WARRINGTON V. PENDLETON BRITANNIA.—There was a capital attendance at Wilderspool on Saturday, when the Warrington "A " easily disposed of Pendleton. In the first half the locals did nearly all the pressing, and tries were scored by Kay, Landor, Jones, Boardman, and Swift, but only one goal was placed, Forster con- verting Boardman 's try. Turning round the visitors were again outclassed, and the game rarely left the Pendleton quarters. Tries were scored by Foster (3), Davies, Landor, Hewitt (3), and Knight. Final score : Warrington, three goals, 12 tries (42 points) ; Pendleton Britannia nil.

ICOMMERCIAL FAlLtrnEsT—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total number of Commercial i failures recorded n England and Wales during the week ending Friday, November 23rd, was 191, viz. : New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 101; Deeds of Arrangement registered, 90. The respective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 99 ; Deeds of Arrangement, 91—total 190; being; an increase of 1. The total num- ber of Commercha Failures recorded during the 47 weeks of the present year is 7,598 ; the total number recorded in the corresponding 47 weeks of last year was 8,140, showing a decrease of 542.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication mnst in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor -aa a guarantee of good faith.

" Cottages at Walton." have read with interest the letter of your •correspondent "Workman" in last Siturday's paper. I should like to say that there are several works which have sprung up in Walton district in the last three years, for the employees of whom no provision has been made. lam employed at Messrs. R. A. Naylor's, Ltd., who employ nine men. The Borate Company employ 20. The Manchester Ship Canal Company when discharging steamers employ 30 to 40 men, and there are sometimes 100 men employed at the Acton Grange lay-by. Then Richard Evans and Co. employ men at their coalyard, besides several other small industries in the neighbour- hood. Most of these men have to walk two miles from Manchester-road, Latchford, etc., to their work, and at the present time there is not a workman's cottage to let in the district. There is no doubt that if a row of say 30 houses at a rental of 4s. per week were erected they would be taken within a week after completion. Three houses erected on the main road were taken before completion.—Yours faithfully, T. M'CORMICK. Gilbert-street, Warrington.

Pauper Labour. Mr. Clay's Explanation. Sir.—Being placed in the unenviable position of hav- in,, to appear as the principal witness in the prosecution of the man Clarke by the Warrington Board of Guar- dians, for failing to support himself, I feel it is my duty to correct the many mis-statements made by persons who cannot have any personal knowledge of the transaction there being only three persons present viz., myself, Clarke and a plasterer named Hind, when the arrangement with this painter was made. Firstly, however, allow me to say that I had no personal animosity against this man as appears to be attributed to me by the Labour Council and the defendant's solicitor. I had no reason to harbour any grudge, as I had not sustained any loss or injury by the man leav- ing. the work he was engaged on. Had I, as is freely , insinuated, wated to get a cheap job "I should have sent a couple of youths to scrape the paper off the walls and not paid sixpence per hour which is equivalent to twenty-nine shillings per week i.e. reckoning the usual hours worked per week. As a matter of fact I sent an unskilled labourer to the job when this man left it who certainly got through vastly more work than did the " journeyman " and as there was still some stripping left when the decorator sent his employees to the job I noticed that it was com- pleted by a youth who, I should guess, would not get more than five shillings a week. This oily corroborated what the man Clarke himself had Enid to me viz. : "That it was a lad's job." Is twenty-nine shillings per week a fair price to pay for work that is usually done by' lads?" It was this statement by Clarke that made me mention tivepence per hour after I had asked him what wages he wanted and he left it to me to state a figure. When he asked for sixpence per hour I at once granted it, as Mrs. Clay had instructed me to start the man and had given me carte blanche as to wages. All this can be corroborated by the other man present, Mr. Hind. No thought of any Union or standard rate of wages (of which I was not aware) ever crossed my mind and even if it had, I should not have expected a Union man to be in the Workhouse as I always understood members of a I7nion received a certain amount of pay out of the funds when out of employment. To enumerate one or two will of the many mis-statements referred to. Firstly, Clarke stated, "he stripped the whole of one shop and the greater part of another." As one of the shops was painted and distempered throughout with the exception of one room of which only a portion was papered, it is easy to be seen that his statement is " impossible " and the small portion was stripped after he left as was also the greater portion of the other shop by the employees previously referred to. His statement " that he has practically painted the whole of the Workhouse" has also as much foundation in fact. Secondly as he was not half-way through the stripping referred to, for which he was properly equipped, there was no necessity for any of the numerous list of painting" brushes enumerated by the defendant's solicitor, as he never saw any paint, nor used any. It was my intention, had the man stuck to his work until the stripping was coin-' pleted to have endeavoured to get him employment on the job by whichever master decorator carried, out the work. The moral of the case, according to the letter sent by the Guardians to the Labour Council, viz. :—" That it was not the practice of the Guardians to allow any member to obtain inmate labour at a lower rate than] the recognised standard" is that all the workmen, of ' which there is a percentage in every trade and who, owing to their incompetence or age or drink, have, drifted into the Workhouse, must be kept by the rate- 1 payers in hoard and lodgings till a master painter comes along and offers them eightpence per hour, or thirty- nine shillings a week, and they cannot be " allowed ' to go out for less. The general body of ratepayers, I think, will sincerely hope this expressed opinion will not be acted upon, or more extensions will be required to the Union.—Yours etc.,

W. A. CLAY.

Park Buildings, Warrington.

Sir.—l always read the letters above the signature of our friend Edmnnd Osborne with interest, and frequently with sympathetic appreciation. But I am afraid I cannot endorse his sentiments on the above subject, although its appearance causes one to think hard. It seems to me the present condition of our Poor Laws, renders it almost impossible for a person,once an inmate of the Workhouse, to get out again and take his place in the ranks of Labour without some assistance of a private or semi-charitable char- acter. They must go in and out within fixed times. We assume they have no means when they are out, or they would not go in, and if they wear pauper I, uniform what possible chance do they stand when making application for work? Is there any means whereby the respective Trade Unions help a man to lift himself from hopeless pauperism to hopeful labour ? I have not heard of those means, and if they do not possess them I am neither blaming or sneering at them, but feel we are then thrown back until those means do exist, upon the opportunities afforded by private and charitable individuals to assist the willing worker, to try once more to take his place amongst his fellow men. And if I judge Mr. Osborne rightly, were he that unfortunate being who wanted the opportunity he would not question too closely the motive but be extremely grateful for the chance to work out his own salvation. Something too has been said about the pittance offered for skilled labour and taking advantage of a man's misfortune. Well I hare been identified with skilled and unskilled labour for more years now than I care to remem- ber and if stripping paper from a wall is skilled labour then I have something more to I earn. And there are scores of men in Warrington doing far more laborious work tinder worse conditions who would be glad to get sd. or 6d. per hour. lam sorry that it is so,but unfortunately it is. Also I hesitate to believe that although there may be some 30 other men out of work in the town,that that fact should be considered sufficient argument to consign another to perpetual pauperism. It is regrettable that we are at all times confronted with the men out of work and the men who want work. And until we reach that happy millenium when there will be work enough for each and all of us, and happiness and content will follow the results of each one's labour, I feel we ought rather to be grateful to those individuals who respond to the solicitations of others, and try to help them upward and onward, and trust that in the present criticisms their part of public service and usefulness will not be readily forgotten. May it be very long before the springs of charity are dried up, is the earnest hope of yours very truly, PATRICK O'BRIEN. 125, Wilderspool-road.

MEXICAN RUBBER.

The Chilian Exploration and Development Syndicate Limited has received unique photographic views taken by Mr. Trencher (chairman of the Syndicate) and Mr. Brewer (director of the Syndicate),on the Syndicate's Castilloa Rubber plantations, State of Oaxaca (the garden spot of Mexico.) These photographs may be seen at the Syndicate's London Offices 20,Bucklersbnry London B.C. In a letter dated on the estates, October 21st, 1906 Mr.Treacher,in recording the first experimental tapping of the rubber trees by Mr. Brewer and himself, states that from Castilloa trees measuring 3ft. 71n. in circum- ference, he took about 30 ounces of rubber latex, which flowed freely, and from which, after coagulation, 12 ounces of rubber of very fine quulity was obtained equal to the best Para Rubber. This was the initial tapping only, and did not include the rubber which over- flowed the incisions made in the tree (scrap rubber). Other tappings of trees 3ft. ilin. to Oft. -lin, in circum- ference, and from 10 to ROft. in height, yielded varying quantities of excellent quality of rubber. Most gratifying results were obtained from the indigenoul Castilloa trees in the plantations, the latex flowed abundantly after tapping, about 130 fluid ounces being taken from one tree alone (without scrap rubber) initial tapping only, and each tree may be tapped at least twice in each yeal, yielding an increasing amount of rubber each time. There were thousands of similar indigenous trees in the immediate forest, besides those indigenous trees amid the plantation, all trees capable of giving :Abs. of the finest rubber, first tapping. Another important feature Mr. Treacher observed was ' numberless rubber saplings amid the plantation, At least 50,000 of which are quite ready to be utilised for extending the plantation. Mr. Brewer enclosed a certi- ficate showing that he had counted 18,710 Castilloa Elastics trees on one of the Syndicate's plantations. Mr. Preacher has sent an important expedition into the vast forests (over 20 square miles in extent, abound- ing in valuable woods, some being suitable for railway sleepers) on the Syndicate's Freehold Land Estates, the expedition's chief object being to ascertain the approximate number of the wild Castilloa trees therein, and to make trial tappings thereof), which expedition would occupy about four weoks.

MR. C. E. PARKER AND THE NON-TRADERS.

REPLY FROM MR. DODDS.

[To THE EDITOR OF TIIF "Eximyke.l Sir,—Mr. Charles E. Parker is more Inimorous than ever in his letter in your last week's issue. If he will only perservere, the dreariness which he says has surrounded or engulfed the Non-trading controversy will have disappeared and soon we shall be holding our sides in the very ecstacies of mirth! Take three points in his letter. (1). He accuses me of having violated the confidence of private intercourse, that is conversation—by having asserted that he had decided against the Non-Traders before he had mastered their platform. The charge of violating the confidence of private intercourse has a dreadful sound about it, until we remember that in this self-same lettcr Mr. Parker had, and without notice or permission, actually produced an opinion of mine expressed to him in a private note upon some of the proposals of the Temperance Legislative League which he had asked me to join ! Even assuming that Mr. Parker has any right to complain, which he had not, as I am about to prove, or even going so far as to admit that two blacks do not make a white, I can still claim that what I said had a direct bearing upon the subject under discussion, while what Mr. Parker has introduced had nothing to do with the controversy, is not at all on parallel lines with it, and was a characteristically thoughtless and possibly a mischievous digression. Let me now refresh Mr. Parker's memory. Early in 1902, at an industrial business meeting near Liverpool, Mr. Parker offensively suggested to me as chairman that a certain commercial arrangement he was approving was contrary to my Non-Trading ideals. I asked him if he understood the Non-Trading principles. He said " No, and I don:t want to."I may add that this is not the only time he had attacked me on this subject at similar meetings, nor the second, nor even the third time. (2) Mr. Parker seems to complain that Non-Traders are lacking in powers of discernment and are unable to comet° correct and logical conclusions in connection with the subject they have at any rate made a special study of for so many years ! But anyone who com- plains on this score ought to give substantial proof that he is himself standing on safe ground. A teacher must be beyond suspicion in the eyes of his scholars. flow, then, stand Mr. Parker's methods and reasoning? In opposite columns I now append (Ist) what I wrote and (2nd) Mr. Parker's version of what I wrote. WHAT I WROTE. MR. PARKER'S VERSION.

"We are more than "Mr. Dodds boasts that thankful to know that the rank and file of both many of the party to political parties are with which they (Mr. Parker him." and his colleagues) belong have been able to see the

rights and wrongs of the controversy and to give

us their

whole-heated

support, just as the rank and file of the Conserva-

tive party have stood by and shown their leaders

which way honour lay." As to Mr. Parker's methods. In the line and a half covering his version of what I wrote, as given above, there are at least two mis-statements. I made no boast, I did not say that the rank and file of both parties were with me, and I did not say that the rank and file of , both parties were even with the Non-Traders. This is pretty good from a man who unctuously calls for "charity" and for a "more excellent way"; who at the same time is seeking to crush a body of men who at personal loss of time and means have brotmht back civic purity to Warrington, and from one who has so shamelessly misquoted me in order to get in another fling at my failure to carry the Latchford seat. Surely the Warrington people know me well enough to be sure that even if they were to desert me on this ques- tion, I should never desert them or their best interests as I conceive them.

Now as to Mr. Parker's reasoning. He says " Mr. Dodds boasts that the rank and file of both political parties are with him. All I need say to this is, that if such is the case the rank and file in the Latchford Ward have a strange way of showing it.' As I have shown I made no such boast, but had I done so I submit with some humility that it is difficult for one to arrive at a just conclusion on any subject unless one takes all the known facts into cbnsideration. If Mr. Parker will look at the way the rank and file of the political parties have done their duty, as reflected by the constitution of the Town Council to-day, he will find that the Non- Traders have more elected members than any other party. "Anyone who cannot in a controversy dis- tinguish between broad results and local incidents must be confused." (The words quoted are Mr. Parker's). (3). Mr. Parker trots out the threadbare and over- used argument that he believes in municipal purity, but he disagrees with the methods that have been adopted to secure purity. The answer to this stock-in-trade balderdash of all our opponents throughout the last six years, is that they have never shown any anxiety to substitude better methods for those we have adopted, and which still hold the field in the absence of the better means which some men in town and some out of it have been dreaming of—and onlydreaming of—for the last half dozen years while we have been hard and seriously at work. So far as I have been able to make out, and I have done my poor best to grasp his mean- ings' Mr. Parker's method would allow the Council to slip back into its bad old ways, and to improve upon its bad old ways, for he would consent to its members receiving not only payment for goods supplied, but for services rendered. That would be quite logical, for the two things go together as a question of right and wrong, but I submit it would scarcely command our admiration as a better method of purifying the Corpora- tion than the one the Non-Traders adopted on the recommendation of a member of His Majesty's present Government and which has enabled them to sweep the traders and all other axe-grinders out of the Council. In regard to Mr. Parker's unique view as to paying members of Councils, I absolutely decline to waste my time in answering a man who, if he would give a inoment's thought to the subject would see that to 'compare the payment of members of Town Councils for services rendered, as such, with a proposed nominal allowance to Members of Parliament in order merely to neutralise differences of distance from Westminster and other inconveniences, and not in any sense for services rendered is nothing more nor less than ridiculous. M.P.'s while Parliament sits give all their time,--many of them live hundreds of miles from London—and the proposed payment is only meant to recoup exceptional outlay just as now, if any Councillor leaves his town, on his town's business, his railway fares, food and lodging, are paid for. There is no intention of paying Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, say at the rate of £5,000 a year. which he would probably consider little enough for his services or £250 to Mr. John Ward which would I believe satisfy him, but simply in all cases to meet expenses which are already met, on the same principle, in segard to members of public bodies. In the sense of paying members of Parliament or members of public bodies for their services, there is nothing to warrant the suggestion of any coming change in the sound public policy which has ever ruled that no personal interest shall accrue to any kind of public representative as the result of his membership of a public body. The Government of to-day and the House of Commons, in reference to the acts of their respective members, are more scrupulously exacting in this regard than ever before and the Municipal Corporations Acts specifically enact that trading or the receiving of profit or payment by members of Corporations renders such members ineligible to hold office. So also in the last Education Act, members are under the same strict rules as members of other public bodies. Mr. C. E. Parker, however, is for displacing all this ; the accumulated experiences of the ages down to this time have no weight with him ; he ;tees forth, let the earth tremble !and let all the people fall on their faces! The truth is, Mr. Parker does not yet understand the A.B.C. of the Non-Trading platform. He says he wants to use your space by keeping to the " main issue" which he takes "to be whether or not the methods of the Non-Traders to establish municipal purity are the best that can be employed." Mr. Parker very well knows that that is not the main issue, although the Non-Traders are quite prepared to defend their methods. I raised the issue, and it was stated by me with, I think, the utmost clearness in the question I put to the Liberal Chairman and party in your issue of the 3rd instant. The Conservatives having come into full line with us ; what were the Liberal Chairman and party goirr,' to do ? I don't admit Mr. Parker's rather disingenlieug claim that he holds in the local Liberal party both the positions held in the local Conservative party by Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr. W. Peter Rylands, namely, president and chairman respectively, though I can quite realise that Mr. Parker is not the man, if he can help it, to brook two Richmonds in any field in which he plays a part. The late Mr. John Itylands and my friend Alderman Pierpoint were for many years respectively Liberal president and chairman. I had a great deal to do with the former, and he invariably spoke of Alder- man Pierpoint as the party chairman. The point is unimportant however,but my appeal was naturally made to the man in the Liberal camp,whose predecessor at the memorable Council meeting of January 7th, 1902, stated in relation to what is now locally known as immoral trading. that it should not be called into question as long as it was legal. The Conservative leader in the Council on that date endorsed the Liberal leader's view, as also did the Council itself.

- Five years have passed ; the present Conservative Chairman, with a straightforwardness such as might might have been expected from a gentleman of the standing of Mr. W. Peter Itylands, came forward on the eve of the recent elections and stated without qualifica- tion that the objection taken in the past by the Non. Traders in respect of trading had been rightly taken. Since the Latchford election and on further reflection, he voluntarily says that no payment should be made to any member of the Council for work done for the Corporation or by the Corporation and that if possible it would be desirable that the Mayor's Auditor should be selected from outside ths ranks of the Corporation. So that so far as the Conservative party in Warrington is concerned, the abstract principle for which I raised

the Non-Trading flag in Latchford has already been triumphantly vindicated, and I am sure that even Mr. Parker will believe that the Conservative acceptance of , the principle I championed is a greater satisfaction to ' me than any merely personal success at the poll could ; have been ! _

If this frank and full, though tardy acceptance of the Non-Trading platform by the Conservatives is a credit to them and a satisfaction to me, the conduct of the Liberal Leaders has throughout been such as to make I me ashamed to be associated with them in the field. Can ! or can I not have a straightforward answer to a straight- forward question from the Chairman of the liberal party in Warrington ? That question is : Do you stand whore your Council leader stood five years ago when he said that immoral trading, if legal should not be called into question, or do you at last admit that the Non-Traders' objection to such trading has all along, as Mr. Rylands says, been rightly taken, and that it shall never again be tolerated in Warrington? Surely the results of the Parliamentary election and of the two last November municipal elections should have opened the eyes of the so-called Liberal leaders. On January 9th last, Mr. A. H. Crosfield wrote me that he was certainly opposed to any member of any public body deriving any profit or payment in any way whatever by reason of such membership, and that he would vote for a bill making any such derivation of profit illegal if done through limited companies or in any other indirect way. Mr. Crosfield was elected. How different the story of the last two municipal elections. Twelve months ago the three contests were between Liberals and Non-Traders ; the latter were easy winners all round. In the election on the first of this month the Liberals did not win a single contest, the Non- Traders and the Conservatives gaining a seat each as compared with three years ago. In short, municipally the Liberal leaders are impotent,and the party is almost hopelessly divided and distracted. Mr. Parker seems to feel slighted that I did not directly appeal to him. As already explained, my question was intended for the successor of Councillor Tinnion in the Council, but had this not been so I should not have gone to Mr. Parker. The Non-Traders were ' at first buffetted and besmirched, they were denounced up hill and down dale. Mr. Parker was against them,of con-se. In 1002 the wind veered round a bit. He began to say there was something in their contention. Then the flag seemed to droop, the Non-Trading leader was defeated at the poll and Mr. Parker comes forward to kindly settle matters and bring peace. This would be all very funny if it were not so serious. Mr. Parker has proved himself in this and other matters a rank opportunist ; hence if the Liberal party is anxious to set itself right in this matter of Non- Trading it will have to be guided by someone who has a more consistent record than Mr. C. E. Parker. Many of the party have allowed themselves to be led by the nose and a fine figure they have cut in consequence, as I have already shown. The leader who is wanted is a man who knows his own opinion and not one who is swayed from side to side by every passing breeze ; a man who says : " If it is right I will support it," and not one whose question constantly is " will it pay ? " or "Will it put me on top? If so lain in favour of it." In a word we need nowadays strong men and not mere wobblers !

I cannot allow Mr. C. E.Parker to step superciliously forward at this time of day and assert that Non-Trading and Municipal Purity are not synonymous terms. Broadly stated the Non-Trading platform is that no one shall prostitute his public position for his private ends and therefore Non-Trading and Municipal Purity are one and the same thing. It is true that the Non- Traders in the Warrington Council have been zealous for the ratepayers' interests in other fields than that strictly covered by the Non-Ting principle. For instance they have worked against and stopped certain ill-advised spending schemes such as the £90,000 for new waterworks at Daresbury and the £11,300 for unneces- sary additions to the Technical Schools. They have also kept their eye upon the notorious Lawson contract with its extras to the tune of £50,000, and they have opposed such property deals as those with Messrs. Birtles, Garnett and Edlesten. These are the methods I presume that Mr. Parker is doubtful about, but what in any case does it all amount to? I am satisfied that in none of these transactions have the interests of the ratepayers been properly safeguarded by the majority of their representatives. If voted twice against the arrangement with Mr. Parker's brother-in- law, Mr. R. E. Garnett, solely because I was never satis- fied in my conscience that it was for the advantage of the general body of the ratepayers of Warrington. I I have found out since what my votes against "interest " have meant! In the Latchford election I had against me all the " interests " I had ever opposed in the Council. I hat' voted with one other Councillor against the sale of the George licence to the brewers. (I wanted it esstinguished.) I was largely responsible for the stopping of all improper trading. My party were against the election of Mr. Luke Ellison to the Aldermanic bench and I had twice voted against the Garnett deal (the unnecessary 40 feet street),and so on the Ist November the big brewers, the Slate Com- pany, Luke Ellison, a brother of Mr. Garnett and brother-in-law of Mr. Parker were all in evidence in the Latchford Ward,professedly in the cause of the right to payment of a Mayor's Auditor and backed by all ths business and family ramifications of those whose interests at one time or another I had had in the Council to oppose. Mr. Parker, so far as I know, was not there in person but he was there in heart and sym- pathy, and he desires it to be known that if he had had a vote he would have been there in person to register it in favour of my opponent. For oh ! Mankind is strangely weak And little to be trusted, If self the wavering balance shakes It's rarely right adjusted. I don't know what you or your readers will think of a man who stood idly by while say a great fire raged, and who, when the conflagation had been got entirely under came forward to find fault with the methods of the men who at the risk of their lives and by strenuous and sustained effort had saved life and property ; the complaint being that the men had used water which though effective for their purpose was not so clean or " excellent "as some their critic knew of ! This has been Mr. Parker's ,attitude and coarse in relation to the greatest moral movement which has been seen in War- rington during the thirty years I have been associated with it. For my part this correspondence with Mr. Parker is at an end. Is it likely that "the battle over, the victory won " lam now going to further shorten the term of my natural life in order to give Mr, Parker some kind of opportunity of further damning me with faint praise and of superciliously discrediting the men who have so heroically backed me. Yours &c., JAMES DODDS. November 29th, 1906.

The Examiner, Saturday, December 1, 1906.

EETTER FROM MR. S. KENYON.

Sir,—l have read Mr. Parker's letter in reference to Latchford Ward Election, Mr. Dodds and other matters. Mr. Parker's remarks about the President of the Liberal Association, Chairman of Committees are not of much public interest, so I need not notice them. The first sentence to which I wish to refer reads as follows : "Non-Trading and Municipal Purity are not synonymous terms and it is quite logical to be doubtful of the one and heartily in sympathy with the other." I BM not aware that any one has ever said that Non-Trading and Municipal Purity are synonymous terms, but is the trading objected to by the Non-Traders desirable ? Does it tend to promote good government and disinterestedness in public life ? That is the important question, and Mr. Parker in his letter has wandered from it. Yet disinterestedness in public life is one and perhaps the most important factor in promoting the welfare of civilized mankind. In other countries the selfishness of public men in public life has produced anarchy, and there is nothing of more importance than to prevent it, and Mr. Parker's refer- ence to the Temperance Legislative League is out of place for no one need have anything to do with that organisation,but ratepayers cannot avoid being connected with the Corporation of the town in which they live. Nor can they without degradation quietly without a protest submit to be misgoverned. The improper pro- ceedings of the Corporation caused the Non-Trading agitation. Let those who find fault with the methods of the Non-Traders improve the methods of the Cor- poration, for that would be a great service to the town. Mr. Parker's political reference to the payment of Members of Parliament is also foreign to the subject, for the Non-Traders have no politics, nor do they care to what party their supporters and members belong. Labour members of Parliament are not paid by the country, but by the organizations which they represent, and no one has anything to say against it. But that is quite a different thing to the payment ef members of Parliament by the country or the payment of Town Councillors by the ratepayers for the one is voluntary and the other would be compulsory. It is also quite a different thing for municipal Corporations to pay Town Councillors for any special service which they may render. In reference to Latch ford Ward election per- haps I may be allowed to state that owing to the illness of the chosen candidate Mr. Dodds was very late in the field. The Ward was not looked after and but little canvassed. Considering these circumstances and all • the influences that were against him' it would have been a marvel if he had been elected. It was with the greatest reluct- ance that Mr. Dodds consented to contest the Ward, and in so doing he made a very great personal sacrifice for a noble cause.

—.--. "Oh, great the hero who wins a name But greater and greater many a time The true and the brave they wrongly blame, For God will finish their work sublime."

S. KENYON.

Social at Great Sankey. A social was held on Friday November 23rd at the Butcher's Arms Hotel in connection with the Sankey St. Mary's Association Football Club. After the mem- bers and friends had partaken of refreshments, songs were given by: Messrs. J. Emson, A. W. Clarke, C. S. Stelfos, A. Appleton, S. Cash, W. Chadwick, 0. Batty, &c., which contributed greatly to the evening's amusement. Mr. Stelfox also gave a few selections on the gramophone, which were much appreciated. Mr. Graley spoke regarding the welfare of the club and hoped that all the members would rally together and make the club a success.

LANCASHIRE CUP FINAL

Warrington's Prospects at Wigan.

LBy " Ovalist."]

The Warrington men are once more in the :final round of a Cup Competition. They have bulked largely in such contests in recent years, and have earned a reputation for prowess equalled by few clubs in the Northern Union. When there is a cup to be fought for, it has usually been the case that Warring- ton have had some say in its ultimate destination, though they have not always brought it to Warring- ton. The present Competition is a new one— originated last season when Wigan became first holders of the Lancashire Senior Cup. Then, War- rington succumbed in the first mama, which has been Wigan's fate this season. To-day's final is to be played on the Wigan ground, Warrington's opponents being the famous Broughton Rangers. The latter have long been recognised as the crack League team, but in Cup Competitions they have not so formidable a record as the Warrington men.

The progress of the two Clubs in the present com- petition is here shown in detail: For Against. _

An analysis of the above figures would suggest that the Rangers have the better defence and are quite Warrington's equal on the attack, and that the odds therefore should be in their favour. Personally, lam inclined to say that, on the season's form and bearing in mind their cap-fighting capabilities the odds should incline to Warrington's side. In any case there is not much to choose between the contesting teams and a rare battle for supremacy is certain. If fine weather only prevails there should be a game worth going miles to sea.

The only matter for regret is that Warrington are not likely to be absolutely at full strength. Efforts to heal the breach with Isherwood were unavailing no to the time the team was selected on Thursday night, and Dickenson retained the position h 5 has filled so well of late. There is only one Isherwood, however, and with him on the team the effectiveness of the famous left wing is ;doubled. Fish always plays better with his old partner and there will be much regret at Isherwood's absence. Of course, seeing that he has been unable to play for over six weeks owing to injury there would have been some risk in selecting him for this important encounter had everything else been "0.K." Dickenson usually plays a grand cup-tie game and if he will not forget that he has the best wing in the Northern Union on his left he should till the bill completely. Fish is reported quite well again, and in better condition than he has been for weeks, while the training reports indicate that the men will be able to go the full eighty minutes at the hottest pace. I have only one qualification to make. If the Warring- ton forwards can make amends for their feebleness at Broughton and beat the Rangers' pack to-day the cup will come to Warrington !

The selected teams are as follows : Warrington :—Berry, full back ; Fish, Dickenson, Taylor and Brooks, threequarters ; Lees and Hocken- hall, half-backs : Preston, Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Harmer and Naylor, forwards. Broughton Rangers : —Barlow, full back ; Hogg, Wilson, C. James and Harris, threequarters ; S. James and Flynn, half-backs ; Mottram Clampitt, Darlison, Whitehead, Beetham, and Ruddick or Oram, forwards. _ _

Referee, 'Mr. J. H. 'Smith (Widnes)—who i hope will be in better form than he was at a certain final between Halifax and Warrington a few years ago !

The Lancashire Senior Cup is a massive one of solid silver, its weight being over 145 ounces. In the centre is a life-like Rugby football scene done in very high relief. On the stem is a shield bearing in brilliant enamels the Lancashire arms in correct heraldic colours. The medals, as will be seen from the illustration are circular in form' haying in the centre the Arms of Lancashire and Cheshire, with a Lancashire Rose above, all done in correct heraldic colours. Round is the inscription. They are of 15ct. gold and both cup and medals have been designed and manufactured by Messrs. l'attorini and Sons, Bradford. -- Warrington's new half-back, who has been playing under the name of Wilson, turns out to be T. 11. Jenkins of Treherbert, South Wales. He has put his signature to the necessary form and I think will prove a useful man to the Club. His display for the "A " team in the last two matches has been con- sistently good. He passes very cleanly and "beats his man" in the most approved style. Jenkins, who was brought here by Shugars, is a sturdily-built fellow, standing 51t., 7in., and scales nearly 11 stone, whilst he has youth on his side, having only just reached his majority.

Warrington did as well as was expected at Hunslet, where they scored ten points against the home team's 10 points. Seeing that such men as Fish, Isherwood and Lees were absent, this was not a bad performance. I did not expect Warrington to win—they very rarely do at Parkside —and their chances were lessened by illness and injury. Hunslet started off in great style and in a quarter of an hour had put together the formidable total of 8 points to Warrington's none. Then the visitors managed to stem the tide and Dickenson galloped over in fine style from a cross- kick by Hockenhall. At half-time there was only five points between the teams.

Hun slet had an advantage for ward and at half-back and not long after resuming they scored again. Warrington had a look in though and Taylor dropped a goal— rather a novelty for the Widnes man. Later on Brooks dashed over at the corner from a pass by Hockenhall and Preston improved. There were prospects of a fight for supremacy at this stage but Jackson put the issue beyond doubt with another try for Hunslet.

The best men on the visiting side were the centres, both of whom played a strong game, while Berry and Brooks also did well. The forwards improved on some recent displays at heeling out, but were rushed off the ball in the loose now and then by the heavy home brigade. Albert Goldthorpe, as usual, played good football and troubled Warrington by his strategy.

On Monday, Warrington made amends for the loss of points at Hunslet by trouncing Swinton handsomely. Of course, they were on their own pet ground again, where they fear no foe in shining armour or any colour of jersey. Neither side was fully representa- tive, but Warrington's reserves proved themselves the most capable. The Blues seemed rather a sorry lot and were shorn of much of their greatness. There was a lot of difference in the weight of the teams and the home forwards had entirely their own way. For a long time Swinton defended valorously and Warrington missed chances by the hair's breadth and other things. Half-time was not very distant when Landor on the left wing scored his first try for the Warrington senior fifteen after a smart round of passing. These three points repre- sented all the scoring up to the interval, though Warrington had been much the superior side.

In the second half the home team's advantage was apparent in the scoring. Once they got going they swamped the Swinton men and points accrued with surprising rapidity. Brooks scored two capital tries which showed his pace and determination to " get there," and Dickenson, Thomas, Jordan, and Board- man one each. Knight and Preston—both of whom had shaped poorly before—kicked a couple of goals each and the final was 4 goals, 7 tries (29 points) to nil. All but three of the points came after half-time and it is thus apparent what a rout of the Blues there was.

The game was not a very classy exhibition, but this could hardly be expected with so many absentees. There were plenty of weak links in the combination eyen of the winners, while that of Swintou was a negligible quantity. The only man who distinguished himself on the visiting side was Dixon the full back who played a capital game against heavy odds. Brooks and Dickenson were the two best three-quarters on the home side, Taylor hardly shiring so much except in defensive work. Landor made a creditable first appearance on the left wing. Knight and Jordan I were erratic, but the latter made up for much by the way in which he combined with Hockenhall in a run from half way for the last try. It was the best bit of work the two halves did. Little need be said of the forwards, seeing that they gave satisfaction by swamping the opposition. Boardman deserves special mention however. It is the first time, I think, that lie has run in a try from half way despite a hot pursuit. He can move, notwithstanding his fourteen stones weight.

Hockey. As expected Brooklands were defeated on the Stockton Heath ground, though the game was of a very dis- appointing character chiefly caused through the ground not being properly prepared. Both sides were minuus good men, Brooklands suffering the more in this respect. The final score was 4-0, the goals being scored by R. Pierpoint, 2 ; S. Baxter,l ; Eric Fair- dough, 1.

The second eleven visited Brooklands, and won by two goals to one. This victory was no fluke being obtained by sound work right through the whole eleven. We hope this is the forerunner of many victories. The county match, Cheshire versus Nottingham, resulted in a win for Cheshire by six goals to one,though the Cheshire eleven were very disappointing, Robson being the only man in the defence to come off." Still it must not be forgotten that Cheshire are searching for "new blood."

The Stockton Heath Ladies Hockey Club opened their season on Wednesday last by playing a match with the Technical School (Girls) eleven at Padgate. The Heathens were not at full strength, being in fact two short, and as the school eleven are a very smart lot, especially their centre forward who is a born player, and keeps the game open in almost a brilliant manner ; the result was never in doubt. The final score was 0-0, but as seven goals were obtained in the first half, it will be seen that the lady "Heathens " were gamo to the last.

TREE PLANTING AT STOCKTON HEATH.

ANNIVERSARY CELEBRATED.

The Smoke Nuisance.

There ware rejoicings at Stockton Heath on Saturday afternoon over the anniversary of tree planting day. They took the form of a public meeting, at which numerous congratulatory speeches were delivered, and the planting of more trees. It is evident that very great interest is taken in the movement by the villagers and there was a large attendance at the public meeting which was held in the Chnrch Institute, Victoria Square, under the presidency of Mr. F. Monks, of Walton Old Hall. Amongst those present were :—The Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Smethurst), Canon Morley Stevenson,Councillor Fulton Smith, Dr. Parker, MessrA. W. E. Bindloss, W. H. Woodcock, C. Plinston, H. J. Westbrook, H. Roberts, G. J. Brown, and A. Fawcett (secretary). Letters of apology for non-attendance were received from 1 Mr. and Mrs. Vere B. Davies, Mr. E. J. Hall, Mr. Arthur Down, Mrs. W. B. Roberts, Mrs. Markel, Mrs. Pendlebury, Alderman and Mrs. Fairolough, and Mrs. A. J. Willett.

The Chairman said he was glad to see that such pro- gress was being made by the movement which had been inaugurated to make Warrington and its suburbs more pleasant and healthy. (Applause.) He did not think manufacturers ought to emit dense volumes of black smoke and smother their neighbours any more than they could help. It was, however, much easier to talk about doing away with smoke than to actually do away with it. As far as he had tried, he could not reoom- mend it to anyone else on purely economical grounds. (Laughter.) For those who had to conduct large works such as those with which he was associated, the time had not yet come when they could do without making a lot of black smoke. They had to do the best they could, and he thought in time they would be able to emit less smoke and a great deal more carbonic acid. Mr. Roberts was connected with a works which made great claims to have achieved economical results from consuming their own smoke. But they were not puddling iron. (Laughter.) They were making soap and chemicals, and he had no doubt they found they could economise with advantage, but it was not so with puddling and making steel. For the present they would have to put up with the nuisance if they liked to call it so, and do the best they could to make their surroundings pleasant. That was the object of the Tree Planting Committee, and of their meeting that day. (Applause.) Mr. Monks then proceeded to give the audience several hints based OD his own experi- ence in gardening and tree planting, and particularly advised them to plant young saplings rather than old trees.

- in--asking the Mayor of Warrington to address the meet- ing the Chairman warmly congratulated Alderman Smethurst on the honour which had been conferred upon him.(Applause.)That was about his first public appearance as chief magistrate and he could not have lent his support to a better object. (Applause.) The Mayor congratulated the people of Stockton Heath on the vitality of the tree planting scheme and said the meeting that day was even larger and more representative than those which were held at Warring- ton in connection with the movement for beautify ing the town. He hoped the efforts they were making to increase the attractiveness of Stockton Heath would be attended with success They were rather rough in Warrington at times' con- tinued the Mayor, but if Stockton Heath were as big as Warrington he almost thought they would be rougher. (Laughter.) The tree-planting had aroused opposition in some quarters, but whatever they did it would not please everybody. The appearance of the village was much improved, and he believed they would in a short time have one of those garden cities which Canon Stevenson was so anxious to promote. The Mayor then alluded to the smoke nuisance, of which he said those who lived in Stockton Heath could have little concep- tion. He realized how difficult it would be to use patent stokers with puddling furnaces, but he thought the out- put of smoke from other sources might be considerably reduced. (Hear, hear.) It was, he believed, largely due to the adoption of mechanical stokers that the Elec- tricity Committee were able to save £l,OOO per annum in their fuel bill. The Mayor concluded by wishing the Tree Planting Society the fullest measure of success. (Applause). Canon Morley Stevenson expressed his sympathy with the efforts they had made so well to beautify Stockton Heath. Of course, in planting trees it was obviously necessary to plant them in rows, but it had been borne in upon him lately that when it was possible to do so, it was a great improvement to plant trees in clumps.He thought they looked better and they sheltered each other from the rough winds. He might illustrate this by referring to that tasteful and beautiful addition which had been made to the corner at Walton by Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall's kindness. (Applause.) There they had in a ring fence a beautiful cluster of trees and shrubs which would in time be a great ornament to the district. With regard to the smoke question, he was perfectly certain it was not a bit of use to use hard words about the people who emitted smoke from factory chimneys,because he did not believe any manufacturer in the town wished to send out black smoke to the annoyance of other people any more than he could help. It was just as unreasonable to say hard' things of a man who in the course of his profession and duty was obliged to send forth a certain amount of smoke, as it would be to accuse a butcher of cruelty because he slew a sheep. (Laughter). He hoped, how- I ever,that the manufacturers would do their best to reduce the amount of black smoke emitted to the greatest possible extent. (Hear, hear.) Many people had spent large sums of money on various experiments and he I could quite conceive that when they heard hard things I said about the emission of black smoke they must feel it rather a hardship. Mr. Monks had been one of the foremost in trying to lessen the amount of smoke which came from the chimneys at his works and he had succeeded to a great extent, although it had not been economical to do it. He (Canon Stevenson) had great faith in the future. He had friends who were scientific people and from talking to them he had reason to believe that the time was not very far distant when the progress of science would enable them to discover methods by which the smoke nuisance might be I very largely diminished if not entirely abolished. He was inclined to say that ten years hence when the people met to celebrate Tree Planting Day they would find that a very great difference had been made in the emission of black smoke and -that the sky would be much clearer in Warrington even than it was on such a beautiful autumn day as that. (Applause.) He hoped the progress of the movement at Stockton Heath would be such that when Warrington people were asked where they were going to spend a holiday the reply would be, "At Stockton Heath, of course." (Laughter and applause). Dr. Parker succeeds in imparting humour to "tree planting" gatherings and on this occasion his address was characteristic.We give some choice passages: —" We are mete" said the doctor "as you all know, to celebrate the anniversary of Tree Planting Day—a day which will ever be regarded as one of the worthiest and most memorable in the history of Stockton Heath. Posterity assembling to celebrate that day with due ceremony and joy, will meet as we do now, but amid what enhaneed I surroundings! As for me, when I look forward into the future, and behold our children in the enjoyment of the full fruit of our labours, when I compare their environment with our own, though I do not envy theta their happiness, I am not without regret that such an environment as they shall possess could not at this moment be ours. But, deprived as we are of the lofty corridor, with its leafy canopy overhead, deprived as we are of the spacious park and its umbrageous groves, as a fit setting and brckground to our present function, we rejoice at the hoaour which is ours alone—the honour of having initiated for the health and entertainment of succeeding generations so beneficient a scheme as this of tree planting. (Applause). Too long have we in this commercial England been blind and callous to the wrongs done by our boasted civilisation to Nature-- Nature who is ever at the disposal of Art, to be ruled and directed by her for the service and delight of man ; and though at last our eyes are open, and we are attempt- ing to undo the misdeeds of the past, we are all too laggard yet in the work of reparation. But, thank Heaven ! a new era has opened, a new spirit is abroad throughout the land, the influence of which is strongly felt by the inhabitants of Stockton Heath. And, ladies and gentlemen, when I reflect that you in this village of Cheshire have been among the first in /your native land to recognise your country's obligations to Nature, and have already returned to her, within the short space of twelve months, as part payment of your debt, no less than 400 trees. (Applause). Having dealt with the formation of The Tree Planting Committee. Dr. Parker continued: " The strong in faith, communicated the good seed, planted by Providence into their own breasts, into the bosoms of others, and lo ! the good seed took toot on every hand and forthwith sprang up into a most prolific crop. But some of that good seed fell on sandy ground—for there is sand in Stockton Heath—(laughter)—and here and there yielded the promise of a tree or portion of a tree; but when the collector in due season went forth to collect there were not even buds of promise found, which proved the sterile nature of the ground. (Laughter). But seine seed had the misfortune to fall upon thorns, and unaccountably stirred up a wasps nest —(laughter)—and such activity of malevolent growth that the young and costly crop was threatened with destruction. But the trees, dear to the hearts of the people grew, notwithstanding, and multiplied and great derangement of mind and loss of equilibrium fell upon the thorns. If you had lost your reason or contracted indigestion in the dissipation and excitement of the times you had suffi- cient provocation but you remained calm, calm in the contemplation of your unselfish and philanthropic labour, and waited patiently for the millenium which you felt was soon to dawn, and if the satisfaction arising from the performance of good deeds is the harbinger of that Era of Peace, then the Millenium was fast approaching, and would soon have shone upon you in unsullied splendour, but alas, those Confounded

Thorns ! They were beginning to make your side sensible of their presence. Those who harboured rancour and resentment in their hearts, with a loud cry of "King's highway in danger" ! raised up a storm, and then all hopes of a Milleniurn sweetly stealing o'er us passed away. You had to prepare, it seemed, for the stress and strife of opposition ere you sat down in peace under your own vine and fig tree; thunder and lightening were in the air, and if the Milleniurn came at all, you would have to ' wait till the clouds rolled • by.' The benefactors of Stockton Heath were called approbrious epithets. They were styled a" Fraternity of Faddists,"" High faluten nonentities" "Innovating upstarts" and "Children of his Satanic Majesty." (Laughter). Their work was denounced as fraulat with mischief, pregnant with suffering for the saints of Stockton Heath. These trees would grow, for ' sooth, and efface the sun's rays by day, and the moon's light by night and Egyptian darkness would settle on the land." (Laughter.) Dr. Parker ' went on to contend that the presence of the trees . tended to improve the health of the neighbourhood and • the occupation of the doctors would be gone. But. • ladies and gentlemen," he continued "ere you despatch me, impoverished and attenuated, to Dutton Poorhouse to die and be no more, let me exhort you, with all the power of my enfeebled voice, in respect of this magni- ficent project of Tree planting, to brace yourselves up. , for one more heroic effort so that completion may . crown your work. Be actuated by the determination to have the whole scheme in the fullest compass realised. Stockton Heath ! join hands with Warrington ! who at your invitation, will willingly advance her leafy standards from Wilderspool to the boundary of your Swing Bridge. Embrace with alacrity and gratitude those wide extending banks proffered you by the generosity of the Ship Canal ! Prolong your avenues to Latchford, to Grappenhall and Lower Walton ! Adorn all your remaining vacant spaces; nor leave derelict thoseside streets,which, if neglected, will always envy your more favoured thoroughfares, while 1 they reproach you for your injustice and neglect ! Do this, and then, but not not till then, can Stockton Heath in real unvarnished truth be styled " The i Beautiful." ( Applause). . Mr. W. E. Bindloss said he believed the people of Stockton Heath had the advantage of living in one of the most charming neighbourhoods near any manufacturing town, but they wanted to make it more beautiful still. (Applause.) On coming home from abroad he was much struck with the squalid surroundings and almost heart-breaking appear- ance of so many thousand English streets in which our operatives lived. If the collective architectural genius of the world had been called together to make some- thing appallingly ugly and loathsome they could not have succeeeed better than they had done in the thousands of rows.of houses and streets in our English manufacturing towns. They wanted to inculcate a love of beauty in the people and the planting of trees would be a step towards that end. (Applause) The Rev. J. S. Hughes said he believed that all was peace in connection with the movement now.(Applause.) He thought they in the neighbourhood of Whitefield- road had been misunderstood in regard to the question. There was no attempt on their part to prevent tree- planting—in fact he thought the institution was one of the noblest in the village. (Applause.) The Society must recognize that some mistakes had been made but that only proved that they were really doing well and they must not be too sensitive to criticism. Mr. Plinston, in moving a vote of thanks to the ladies who had consented to plant trees, and to the Stockton Heath Forge Company for presenting spades, said he believed all little differences and bickerings were over, . and they were prepared to work harmonionsly together. (Applause). Mr. Westbrook seconded, and the vote was heartily accorded. Spades were presented to the following ladies who had promised to plant trees :—The Mayoress, Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mrs. Henry Roberts, Mrs. William Richardson, Mrs. Hughes, Miss Broadbent, Miss Greenall, Miss Plinston Mrs. Fawcett, Mrs. John Fair- hurst and Mrs. Woodcock. Miss Sylvia Broadbent responded to the vote of thanks and expressed the hope that Latchford would emulate the good example set by Stockton Heath, and that possibly the Garden Society might extend its operations to that district. (Applause). She hoped the movement to improve the physique of the people by universal military training would also receive their sympathy and be carried on along with the beautifying movement, as the two would go together splendidly. (Applause.) A vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman on the motion of Mr. G. J. Brown seconded by Mr. H. N. . Houghton. 1 It had been intended to plant more trees in ) Grappenhall-road but the arrangements could not be completed in time, and trees were therefore planted in ' Cavvdor-street in the presence of a very large gathering.

SOAP COMBINE COLLAPSE.

Official Statement.

The following official statement has been issued : "The working arrangement entered into between the leading soap makers of the United Kingdom has beee received with such great disfavour by the trade and the public as to make it unworkable, and is it is clearly the first consideration to endeavour to satisfy the trade and the public, it has been decided to terminate the working arrangements from to-day (November 23rd). Each firm will, therefore, continue to conduct from this day its own business, as was done prior to the negotiations being entered upon, and entirely separate from each other. The working arrangement between the leading soap makers is now, therefore, absolutely and finally dissolved."

The parties to the amalgnmation were : Messrs. Joseph Ciosfield and Sons, Warrington. Messrs. Watson and Sons, Leeds. Messrs. Lever Brothers, Port Sunlight. Messrs. William Gossage and Sons, Widnes. Messrs. Tyson, Richmond, and Jones, Liverpool. Messrs. Hodgson and Simpson, Wakefield. Messrs. Edward Cook and Sons, London. Messrs. Christopher Thomas and Bros., Bristol. Messrs. J. Barrington and Co., Dublin. Messrs. Ogston and Tennant, Glasgow and Aberdeen 1 and Vinolia (Limited). MR. CROSPIELD, M.P., ON THE PUBLIC'eLOSS. Mr. A. H. Crosfleld, M.P., has made the following statement in an interview with a Press representative : —There can be no question that the cost of nroduction of a large proportion of soap manufactured in the I United Kingdom would have been reduced. Public opinion has been misled by sensational statements, and as our customers and their customers seem indifferent to the cost of production, we must cheerfully' accept the situation, though I think personally the arrange- ment would have been for the benefit of producers and consumers as a whole. Capable small makers were not oppressed under the rbgime of old soap association, neither would they have been oppressed under the new arrangement. The alleged "squeezing of the public," another statement intended to tickle the ears of the gallery, is absolutely without foundation. It is amusing to see that Mr. Arthur Chamberlain, who has been denouncing what he innocently- but foolishly called a "trust," now responds that one could not be broken up lat a single meeting. Of course, but it is no less amus- ing to see him plaintively complaining that this working arrangement is abandoned. Yet only the other day he was boasting of his individualism.

MESSRS. CROSFIELDS' CIRCULAR TO CUSTOMERS. Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Limited,Warring- ton, have issued the following circular to their customers : Dear sir,—We beg to advise you that the working arrangement entered into between ourselves and some of the leading soaprnakers of the United Kingdom has been received with such great disfavour by customers and the public as to make it unworkable, and as it is clearly our first consideration to endeavour to satisfy customers and the public, it has been decided to terminate this working arrangement as from to-day. We shall therefore continue to conduct, from to-day, our business as was done prior to the negotiations being entered into, and entirely separate from each Other. The working arrangements between the leading soap- makers in the United Kingdom is now therefore abso- lutely and finally dissolved.—Yours faithfully, Jos. CROSFIELD St SONS, LTD. 24th November, 1906. In a telegram to the " Dilly Dispatch," Messrs.. Crosfleld deny the existence of any secret understand- ing between them and any other firm. " What can we do more than declare emphatically that our company is absolutely and entirely separate and that no secret understanding exists ?" they ask.
